H
is abelian (see Remark
4.17
); in particular, a parachute is not always a simple étage.

Remark 4.28
.

If
G
is an extended tower
over
H
, there
is a retraction from
G
to
H
, so one may view
H
both as a subgroup and a quotient of
H
.
In general, however,
H
is not canonically defined as a subgroup of
G
; for instance, changing the choice of the conjugates
\tilde{B}_{i}
’s in Proposition
4.21
expresses
G
as an étage
over isomorphic, but non-conjugate, subgroups.
What is canonical is the family of homomorphisms

B_{\Gamma}\rightarrow G
which agree with a conjugation on each
B_{i}
.
Depending on context, one may want to think
of
H
either as a subgroup of
G
, or as an abstract group, forgetting about its embedding in
G
. Roughly speaking, Sections
6
and
7
are about abstract groups, Section
8
about subgroups.

Remark 4.29
.

When
n=1
and
B_{1}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
(
G
is a parachute),
the existence of the retraction
G\rightarrow\tilde{B}_{1}*\langle t\rangle
in Proposition
4.21
is equivalent to the existence of a
retraction
\tilde{\rho}:G*{\mathbb{Z}}\to B_{1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\tilde{\rho}(Q)
is not abelian.
This is the way Perin defines it in
[
Per13
]
.
Note in particular that
G*{\mathbb{Z}}
is a simple étage of surface type over
B_{1}*{\mathbb{Z}}\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

The following remarks apply to both simple and extended étages/towers.

Remark 4.30
.

If
G
is an étage over
H
, then
G*G^{\prime}
is an étage over
H*G^{\prime}
for any
G^{\prime}
(replace the bottom group
B_{1}
of
\Gamma
by
B_{1}*G^{\prime}
).
It follows that, if
G
is a tower over
H
and
G^{\prime}
is a tower over
H^{\prime}
, then
G*G^{\prime}
is a tower over
H*H^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.31
.

In a tower, one can switch étages of surface type and of free product type as follows:
if
G_{i-1}=G_{i}*{\mathbb{Z}}
and
G_{i}
is an étage of surface type over
G_{i+1}
,
then
G_{i-1}
is an étage of surface type over
G^{\prime}_{i}=G_{i+1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is an étage of free product type over
G_{i+1}
. This allows us to assume that all étages of surface type are at the top of the tower.

Proposition
4.21
is about single étages. The following is about towers.

Proposition 4.32
.

Assume that
G
is an extended tower over a subgroup
H
.

Then there are subgroups
G=G_{0}>G_{1}>\dots>G_{k}=H^{\prime}
,
a (possibly trivial) free group
F\subset H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}=H*F
, and for
i<k
non-exceptional
retractable centered splittings
\Gamma_{i}

of
G_{i}
, and
retractions
\rho_{i}:G_{i}\rightarrow G_{i+1}
such that:

•

the image under
\rho_{i}
of the central vertex group
Q_{i}
of
\Gamma_{i}
is
non-abelian;

•

for
i>0
, the group
G_{i}
is the free product of some conjugates of the bottom groups of
\Gamma_{i-1}
, plus an extra cyclic factor if
i=k
and
\Gamma_{k-1}
is simple with an abelian base.

Proof.

Using Remark
4.31
, starting from a sequence of étages defining the tower, one
can move the étages of free product type down to get
a sequence of groups

G=G_{0},G_{1},\dots,G_{k}=H^{\prime}
,
where each
G_{i}
is an étage of surface type over
G_{i+1}
and
H^{\prime}=H*F
with
F
free.
We conclude by applying Proposition
4.21
. If
G_{k}
is abelian, we must enlarge
G_{k}=H^{\prime}
and
F
by taking their free product with
\langle t\rangle
as in Proposition
4.21
.
∎

Remark 4.33
.

Assume for simplicity that
G
is hyperbolic (or more generally that all abelian subgroups of
G
are cyclic).
The subgroups appearing in this proposition are almost the same as those in Definition
4.26
, but not exactly, because of
parachutes: a parachute is an étage over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, but in order to have a retraction such that the surface group has non-abelian image one must retract to a free group of rank 2.
Conversely, if there are subgroups
G_{i}
as in
the proposition, then
G
is an extended tower over
H
, except possibly if
H=G_{k}\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
: in this case it may happen that
G
is a tower over a cyclic free factor of
H
, but not over
H
itself.

Figure 9:
Top part: non-exceptional surfaces except
N_{4}
are simple étages over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
(the top right picture is not a valid étage because
N_{2,1}
is exceptional).

Bottom part:
N_{4}
is an extended étage in two different ways.

The following lemma implies that a given
G
cannot be a tower of arbitrary length.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
G
be a torsion-free CSA group. Denote by
b_{1}^{2}(G)
the first Betti number modulo 2 (the dimension of
\mathrm{Hom}(G,{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})
over
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
).
If
G
is an extended étage over
H
, then
b_{1}^{2}(G)>b_{1}^{2}(H)
.

Proof.

The result is clear if the étage is of free product type. Assume it is of surface type, associated to a surface
\Sigma
and a centered splitting
\Gamma
. Since
H
is a retract of
G
, it suffices to find a homomorphism from
G
onto
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}

vanishing on
H
.
This is easy to do
if
\Sigma
is not planar or if
\Gamma
is not a tree of groups.
In the remaining case (
\Sigma
is planar and
\Gamma
is a tree),
\Gamma
cannot be retractable
[
GLS19
]
: a retractable
\Gamma
with
\Gamma
planar has no terminal vertex (see Remark
4.25
).
∎

Recall that
S_{g,b}
and
N_{g,b}
denote the orientable/non-orientable
surfaces of genus
g
with
b
boundary components, and
S_{g}=S_{g,0}
,
N_{g}=N_{g,0}
.

Example 4.35

(Closed surface groups, see Figure
9
)
.

All non-exceptional closed surface groups except
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
are simple étages over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, and
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
is an extended étage over
{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

We sketch the proof (compare
[
Per11
,
LPS13
]
).
Every
S_{g}
with
g\geq 2
is non-exceptional and
\pi_{1}(S_{g})
is a simple étage over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
(see Figure
9
).
For
g\geq 5
, decomposing
N_{g}
into
N_{g-2,1}
and
N_{2,1}
makes
\pi_{1}(N_{g})
a simple étage over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
When
g=4
, this does not yield an étage structure because
N_{2,1}
is exceptional.

To express
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
as an extended étage,
view

N_{4}
as a twice-punctured Klein bottle
N_{2,2}
with a Möbius band
glued to each boundary component.
This yields a retractable splitting of its fundamental group

\pi_{1}(N_{4})
with base
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, so
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
is an extended étage over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
(the associated retraction maps
\pi_{1}(N_{4})=\langle a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2}\mid a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}=b_{1}^{2}b_{2}^{2}\rangle
to
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
by sending
b_{i}
to
a_{i}
).
One may also decompose
N_{4}
as the union of

N_{3,1}
and a Möbius band, so
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
has a simple retractable splitting with base
{\mathbb{Z}}
(but is not a simple étage, see Remark
4.17
).

The surface
N_{3}
is exceptional, its fundamental group is not an extended étage.

4.6
Relative extended towers

Recall that, if
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
H
, associated to a centered splitting
\Gamma
of
G
, the bottom groups are by definition elliptic in
\Gamma
and are well-defined up to conjugacy as subgroups of
G
. On the other hand, if the étage is not simple, there is no canonical embedding of
H
into
G
.
This motivates the following definition, which is important for applications.

Definition 4.36

(Relative extended tower
[
Per11
]
)
.

Let
G=G_{0}>G_{1}>\dots>G_{k}=H
be an extended tower.
The tower is
relative to a subgroup
A\subset H
if
each étage of surface type is relative to
A
in the following sense:
the group
A
is contained, up to conjugacy, in a bottom group of the centered splitting defining the étage.

Example 4.37
.

Any simple tower over
H
is relative to
H
, or to any subgroup
A\subset H
.
Now suppose that
G
has a retractable centered splitting
with two bottom groups
B_{1},B_{2}
and base
G_{1}=B_{1}*B_{2}
.
Also suppose that
G_{1}
is a simple étage over
H
. The tower
G>G_{1}>H
is relative to
H
if and only if
H
is contained in a conjugate of
B_{1}
or
B_{2}
.

Remark 4.38
.

If
G
is an étage of free product type over
H<G
, i.e.
G=H*{\mathbb{Z}}
, then the
étage is automatically relative to
A
for any subgroup
A<H
.

Remark 4.39
.

Proposition
4.32
holds if
G
is an extended tower over
H
relative to
A
, with
A
contained in a conjugate of a bottom group of
\Gamma_{i}
for
i=0,\dots,k-1
.

Remark 4.40
.

If
G_{i}
as in Definition
4.36
is an extended étage of surface type over
G_{i+1}
, then
G_{i+1}
is the free product of the bottom vertex groups of
\Gamma_{i}
. Thus,
if
A<H
is a one-ended subgroup,
the tower is automatically relative to
A
.
More generally, if

A
is
one-ended relative to a subgroup
A^{\prime}
, and the tower is relative to
A^{\prime}
, then the tower is also relative to
A
.

Remark 4.41
.

An extended étage of surface type over
H
is relative to
H
if and only if it is simple.
If
G
is an extended tower over
H
relative to
H
, then the last étage (between
G_{k-1}
and
G_{k}
) has to be an étage of free product type or a simple étage.

4.7
Relation with other definitions

Sela introduced hyperbolic
\omega
-residually free towers in
[
Sel01
]
.
They are defined as simple towers (involving only étages of surface type) over a (non-abelian)
free product of (maybe 0) non-exceptional closed surface groups and a (possibly trivial) free group.
Because étages of free product type may be moved to the bottom by Remark
4.31
,
one does not change the definition if one allows étages of free product type in this definition.
Moreover,
since fundamental groups of non-exceptional surfaces except
N_{4}
are simple towers over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
(Example
4.35
),
and since
\pi_{1}(N_{4})*\pi_{1}(N_{4})
and
\pi_{1}(N_{4})*{\mathbb{Z}}
are simple towers over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,

\omega
-residually free towers coincide with simple towers over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
or over
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
.

Our extended towers are almost identical to the extended hyperbolic towers defined by Perin in
[
Per13
]
.

Perin defined hyperbolic floors/étages in
[
Per11
]
allowing the possibility of
a non-simple centered splitting, i.e. a splitting having several bottom vertices.
In
[
Per13
, Section 4]
Perin alludes to the possibility that Sela also thinks of non-simple floors/étages,
but this definitely does not appear in print.

As mentioned in Remark
4.22
, Perin’s definition needed a fine tuning, hence the introduction of extended hyperbolic floors in
[
Per13
]
. We do not
use the same retraction as Perin
in the case of parachutes, but this makes no difference
(see Remark
4.29
).

Unlike Perin, we insist that only one surface appears in each étage (we only use centered splittings). This does not affect the definition of towers, though it increases the number of étages in a given tower. We do not use Bass-Serre presentations, but we select conjugates of the
B_{i}
’s in Proposition
4.21
.

Perin does not consider étages of free product type, but by Remark
4.31

G
is an extended tower over
H
in our sense if and only if
G
is an extended hyperbolic tower over
H*F
in the sense of Perin
[
Per13
]
for some
free subgroup
F
.

We now quickly review the definition of regular NTQ systems and NTQ groups from
[
KM05
, Section 7.6]
.
If
{\mathcal{S}}=\{S_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\dots,S_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\}
is a set of equations in the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
(maybe with constants in
G
),
the coordinate group
G_{R({\mathcal{S}})}
of
{\mathcal{S}}
is the largest residually-
G
quotient of
\langle G,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
,
i.e. the quotient of

G*\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle
by
the set of words
r(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
(with constants in
G
) such that
any
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in G
satisfying
S_{i}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})=1
also satisfies
r(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})=1
.
We will restrict to the case where
G
is a limit group (over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
).

The building blocks of regular NTQ systems are quadratic systems of equations (corresponding to surface groups),
and empty equations (corresponding to the free product with a free group).
More generally, a TQ system, as defined in Definition 43 of
[
KM05
]
,
also allows commutation equations (corresponding to extensions of centralizers, or abelian étages),
but these are ruled out in the definition of
regular
NTQ systems.
In regular NTQ systems, one requires
[
KM05
, Def. 6]
that the quadratic equations correspond to non-exceptional surfaces
and that the equations have a non-commutative solution in
G
(corresponding to retractions such that the surface groups have non-abelian image).
Thus, a group is the coordinate group
G_{R({\mathcal{S}})}
of a regular quadratic system of equations over
G

if and only if it is a simple étage of surface type over
G
.
A group
L
is the coordinate group of a regular NTQ system over
G
(including constants) if and only if it
is a simple tower over
G
.
We note that, for
G={\mathbb{F}}_{r}
with
r\geq 2
, this class of groups depends on
r
(all such groups have an epimorphism to
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
).
For
r=2
, this class includes the fundamental group of all non-exceptional surfaces except
N_{4}
.

If a system of equations
{\mathcal{S}}
does not involve constants in
G
, one can additionally define
a
constant-free
coordinate group
G^{*}_{R(S)}

as the largest residually-
G
quotient of

\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid{\mathcal{S}}\rangle.

A group
L
is the constant-free coordinate group of a regular NTQ system without constants over the free group
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}

if and only if
L*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
.
One can easily check that this notion does not depend on
r\geq 2
.
This yields a larger class of groups, containing all non-exceptional surface groups (including the fundamental group of
N_{4}
), elementarily free parachutes,

{\mathbb{Z}}
and the trivial group.

5
Some properties of towers

In this section we assume that
G
is CSA, torsion-free, finitely generated.

5.1
Making an étage simple

Simple towers are better than extended towers; in particular, Theorem
2.1
about elementary embeddings only applies to simple towers.
It is therefore desirable to make an étage simple.
We show that this is possible if the surface is complicated enough, or if we are prepared to enlarge
G

by taking a free product with a free group (Corollary
5.4
).

Proposition 5.1
.

Assume that
G
has a
non-simple

retractable centered splitting
\Gamma

such that
the associated surface
\Sigma
has Euler characteristic satisfying
\chi(\Sigma)\leq-3
.

Then
G
has a retractable
simple
splitting
\hat{\Gamma}
whose base is isomorphic to that of
\Gamma
(so
G
is a simple étage of surface type).

The proposition applies unless the non-exceptional surface
\Sigma
is a 4-punctured sphere
S_{0,4}
, a thrice-punctured projective plane
N_{1,3}
, or a twice-punctured Klein bottle
N_{2,2}

(the twice-punctured torus cannot appear in a non-simple retractable splitting by
[
GLS19
]
, see Remark
4.25
).
These surfaces cause pathologies that will be studied in Section
10
.

Figure 10:
Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Proof.

Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
be the bottom groups of
\Gamma
, with
n\geq 2
, and
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
the central group.
Let
p:Q\to G
be a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map. Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two boundary components of
\Sigma
whose fundamental groups are contained in conjugates of
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
respectively (see Figure
10
).
Using Lemma
4.9
, we may find a (possibly non-maximal) family
{\mathcal{C}}
of pinched curves separating
\Sigma
into two connected surfaces
\Sigma^{1},\Sigma^{2}
containing
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
respectively (on Figure
10
, the family
{\mathcal{C}}
consists of the single curve
\delta
).

We choose an arc
\gamma\subset\Sigma
joining
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
and meeting
{\mathcal{C}}
exactly once, and we call
\delta
the curve of
{\mathcal{C}}
which meets
\gamma
.
We view
Q
as
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,x)
, with a basepoint
x\in\gamma
, and using
\gamma
to join
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
to
x
we can view
\pi_{1}(C_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(C_{2})
as subgroups of
Q
which are well-defined, not just up to conjugacy (this is similar to the choice of subgroups
\tilde{B}_{i}
in the proof of Proposition
4.21
).

Let
P\subset\Sigma
be a pair of pants obtained as a regular neighborhood of
C_{1}\cup\gamma\cup C_{2}
, with
\partial P
consisting of
C_{1},C_{2}
, and a third curve
C
.
We shall define a boundary-preserving map
\hat{p}
from
\pi_{1}(\hat{\Sigma})
to
G
, where
\hat{\Sigma}
denotes the surface obtained from
\Sigma
by removing
P
. Note that
\chi(\hat{\Sigma})=\chi(\Sigma)+1\leq-2
, so
\hat{\Sigma}
is non-exceptional.

A regular neighbourhood of
C_{1}\cup\gamma\cup C_{2}\cup\delta
is a
4
-punctured sphere containing
P
; its boundary consists of
C_{1},C_{2}
and
curves
C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}
with
C^{\prime}_{i}\subset\hat{\Sigma}\cap\Sigma^{i}
.
Since
\delta
is pinched, the image of
\pi_{1}(C^{\prime}_{i})
by
p
is conjugate to that of
\pi_{1}(C_{i})
, hence contained in a conjugate of
B_{i}
.

We cannot directly use the restriction of
p
to
\pi_{1}(\hat{\Sigma})
because it does not have to map
\pi_{1}(C)
to a conjugate, so we first have to modify
p
.
Denote by
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
the pinched space (see Definition
4.8
), so that
p
factors through
p_{\mathcal{C}}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow G
. Let
\Sigma^{1}_{\mathcal{C}},\Sigma^{2}_{\mathcal{C}}
be the images of
\Sigma^{1},\Sigma^{2}
in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
, and denote by
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}
the restriction of
p_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{i}_{\mathcal{C}})
.
Since
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{1}_{\mathcal{C}})*\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{2}_{\mathcal{C}})
is a free factor
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
, one can modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
by composing each
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}
with a conjugation so that the modified map
p^{\prime}=p_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{C}}
from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to
G
is a boundary-preserving map which is the identity on
\pi_{1}(C_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(C_{2})
, hence on
\pi_{1}(P)
.

The restriction
\hat{p}
of
p^{\prime}
to
\pi_{1}(\hat{\Sigma})
is a boundary-preserving map. It is associated to a splitting
\hat{\Gamma}
having one less vertex group than
\Gamma
: the vertex groups
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
have been replaced by a single vertex group isomorphic to
B_{1}*B_{2}
, in particular the base of
\hat{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to that of
\Gamma
.
The non-degeneracy of
\hat{p}
follows from the fact that its image contains those of the groups
\pi_{1}(C^{\prime}_{i})
, which are non-trivial and contained in conjugates of
B_{i}
.

If
\hat{\Gamma}
is not simple, we can
iterate this process and obtain a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map associated to a simple splitting. There remains to check that the corresponding surface
\Sigma_{f}
is non-exceptional when
\chi(\Sigma)\leq-3
.

We denote by
g,b,n
the genus of
\Sigma
, the number of boundary components of
\Sigma
, and the number of bottom vertices of
\Gamma
respectively. Retractability of
\Gamma
implies
g+b\geq 2n
(
[
GLS19
]
, see Remark
4.25
).
By construction
\Sigma_{f}
has genus
g
and
b-n+1
boundary components. We assume that
\Sigma_{f}
is exceptional (so
\chi(\Sigma_{f})=-1
and
g\leq 2
),
and we deduce that
\chi(\Sigma)=-2
.

First suppose that
\Sigma
is orientable. This implies
g=0

since non-planar orientable surfaces are non-exceptional. Then
b\geq 2n
and

1=|\chi(\Sigma_{f})|=b-n-1\geq n-1
, so
n=2
,
b=4
, and
\Sigma
is a 4-punctured sphere.

If
\Sigma
is non-orientable with
g=1
, we have
1=|\chi(\Sigma_{f})|=b-n\geq n-1
, so
n=2
,
b=3
, and
\Sigma
is a thrice-punctured projective plane.

If
\Sigma
is non-orientable with
g=2
, we have
1=|\chi(\Sigma_{f})|=b-n+1\geq n-1
, so
n=b=2
and
\Sigma
is a twice-punctured Klein bottle.
∎

Figure 11:
If
G
is an extended étage with bottom groups
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
,
B_{3}
, then
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is a simple étage over a subgroup isomorphic to
B_{1}*B_{2}*B_{3}
.

Proposition 5.2

(See Figure
11
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
.
If
it is retractable,
there exists a (possibly trivial)
free group
F
and a simple retractable splitting
\Gamma_{s}
of
G*F
,
which makes
G*F
a simple étage.

The splittings
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{s}
are associated to the same surface. The base of
\Gamma_{s}
is isomorphic to the base of
\Gamma
, except that
\Gamma_{s}
has base
B_{1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\Gamma
is simple with an abelian base
B_{1}
.

Conversely, we will see
(Corollary

5.18
) that
G
has a retractable splitting whenever some
G*F
has one.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove if
n=1
, except if
\Gamma
has an abelian base
B_{1}
. But then
G*{\mathbb{Z}}
is a simple étage over
B_{1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Now assume
n\geq 2
(see Figure
11
). Let
\rho:G\to H=\tilde{B}_{1}*\dots*\tilde{B}_{n}
be a retraction provided by Proposition
4.21
.
Write
{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}=\langle t_{2},\dots,t_{n}\rangle
and define
\varphi:H\to G*{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}
as being the identity on
\tilde{B}_{1}
and conjugation by
t_{i}
on
\tilde{B}_{i}
. It induces an isomorphism (also denoted by
\varphi
) between
H
and a subgroup
H^{\prime}=\tilde{B}_{1}*\tilde{B}_{2}^{t_{2}}*\cdots*\tilde{B}_{n}^{t_{n}}\subset G*{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}
, with
\tilde{B}_{i}^{t_{i}}=t_{i}\tilde{B}_{i}t_{i}^{-1}
.

The group
H^{\prime}
is the base of a simple
centered splitting of
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}
, whose restriction to
G
is
\Gamma
. The map

\rho^{\prime}:G*{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}\to H^{\prime}
equal to
\varphi\circ\rho
on
G
and killing every
t_{i}

is a retraction from
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{n-1}
to
H^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

If
\Gamma

is not simple, this construction makes
G*F
a simple étage over a subgroup

H^{\prime}
which is isomorphic to
H
but is not contained in
G
.

Corollary 5.4
.

If
G
is an extended tower over a group
H
,
there exists a free group
F
such that
G*F
is a simple tower over a subgroup
H^{\prime}
isomorphic to
H*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. If
H
is not abelian, one can take
H^{\prime}\simeq H
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.32
,
there exists a tower
G=G_{0}>G_{1}\dots>G_{k}=H*F^{\prime}
where
G_{i}
is an extended étage of surface type over
G_{i+1}
and
F^{\prime}
is free.

Assume first that
H
is not abelian.
Then no
G_{i}
is abelian, so for all
i<k
there is a free group
F_{i}
such that
G_{i}*F_{i}
is a simple tower over a subgroup
G^{\prime}_{i+1}
isomorphic to
G_{i+1}
.
Applying Remark
4.30
inductively, one finds a free group
F
such that
G*F
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
H*F^{\prime}
, hence over
a subgroup isomorphic to
H
.

If
H
is abelian, then

G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

is an extended tower over the non-abelian group
H*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}

by Remark
4.30
, and the argument given above applies.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In Corollary
5.4
,
assume that the tower is relative to a subgroup
A<H
,
and let
H=H_{1}*\dots*H_{k}*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
be a Grushko decomposition of
H
relative to
A
. At each étage, every
H_{j}
is contained in a conjugate of a bottom group.
It follows from the way that
\varphi
was defined in the proof of Proposition
5.2
that there is an isomorphism
\psi:H\to H^{\prime}
whose restriction to each
H_{j}
agrees with the conjugation by some element of
G*F
(depending on
i
);
in particular
H^{\prime}
contains a conjugate of
A
(and we may choose
H^{\prime}
so that it contains
A
). If the tower is relative to
H
itself, then
H^{\prime}
is conjugate to
H
in

G*F
.

By Remark
4.40
,
the tower is automatically relative to
A
if
A<H
is one-ended; thus
any
one-ended
A<H
is contained in
H^{\prime}
up to conjugacy.

This also holds if
A
is one-ended relative to a subgroup
A^{\prime}<A
and the tower is relative to
A^{\prime}
.

The following lemma will be used in Section
9
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume that
G
is an extended tower over a subgroup
H<G
relative to
A<H
, and let
H=H_{1}*\dots*H_{k}*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
be the Grushko decomposition of
H
relative to
A
.

Then
(H_{1},\dots,H_{k})
is a malnormal family in
G
: if
H_{i}^{g}\cap H_{j}\neq\{1\}
, then
i=j
and
g\in H_{i}
.

Proof.

First assume that the tower
G=G_{0}>G_{1}>\dots>G_{n}=H
is simple.
Then
G_{i}
is malnormal in
G_{i-1}
for each
i\geq 1
by Remark
4.4
. It follows that
H
is malnormal in
G
,
and the lemma follows from the malnormality of the family of Grushko factors of
H
.

We now turn to the general case.
By Corollary
5.4
and Remark
5.5
,
G*F
is a simple tower over a group
H^{\prime}
, and there is an isomorphism from
H
or
H*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
to
H^{\prime}

whose restriction to each
H_{j}
is inner. The result follows from the case of a simple tower.
∎

5.2
Narrowing the range

In this subsection we consider a subgroup
H\subset G
and a splitting of
H
. We assume that the splitting is retractable in
G
(Definition
4.23
), and we wish to show that it is retractable in
H
. Our results are based on the following lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\subset H\subset G
, with

H
malnormal.
Let

p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
be a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map,
and let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a maximal family of pinched curves, with
{\mathcal{C}}\neq\emptyset
.

Assume that, whenever
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}
(as in Definition
4.8
) contains a component of
\partial\Sigma
, the image of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
is contained in a conjugate of
H
. Then there is a pinching non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to H
.

Proof.

We factor
p
into
p=p_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{C}}
with
\pi_{\mathcal{C}}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
and
p_{\mathcal{C}}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})\to G
(see Definition
4.8
).
The group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
is isomorphic to a free product whose factors are closed surface groups, cyclic groups, and the
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
’s for which
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}
contains a component of
\partial\Sigma
; we fix such an isomorphism.
We shall modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
on each free factor, and
p^{\prime}
will be
p_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{C}}
.

We can modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
on each
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
, by composing with a conjugation of

G
, so that the image of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
is contained in
H
(rather than a conjugate); by malnormality of
H
, the modified
p_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{C}}

acts on each boundary subgroup of
\Sigma
as
conjugation by some element of
H
.
We can also modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
on the other free factors so that the image of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
is contained in
H
.
We now have to make sure that this
image
is non-abelian.

If
\Sigma
has two boundary components
C_{1},C_{2}
belonging to different surfaces
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j_{1}}
,
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j_{2}}
, the images of
A_{1}=\pi_{1}(C_{1})
and
A_{2}=\pi_{1}(C_{2})
are conjugate to subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
(in
G
hence in
H
by malnormality);
we
can modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j_{1}})

so that
p_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{1})
and
p_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{2})
are two non-commuting conjugates of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in
H

(for instance by mapping
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
to themselves in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\subset H
, since they do not commute there).

If
\partial\Sigma
is contained in a single
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}
, and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
has abelian image, we argue as in the proof of Proposition
4.14
. Recall that
G
is torsion-free and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
has non-abelian image under the original
p_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Consider the factors in the decomposition of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}})
as a free product. Besides
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})
, there must be at least a
{\mathbb{Z}}
or the group of a closed surface different from
S^{2}
and
P^{2}
. Such a surface group maps onto
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and we can modify
p_{\mathcal{C}}
to make its image non-abelian.
∎

We can now state:

Proposition 5.8
.

Assume that

H
is a free factor of
G
.
If a splitting of
H
is retractable in
G
, then it is retractable in
H
.

We view
this as a special case of the following more precise result, which will be needed later.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\subset H\subset G
, with

H
a free factor of
G
.
If there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
,
there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to H
.
Moreover,
p^{\prime}
is pinching or
p^{\prime}=\iota\circ p
with
\iota
an inner automorphism of
G
.

Proof.

If
p
is non-pinching, its image is contained in a conjugate of
H
by Lemma
3.7
and we take
p^{\prime}=\iota\circ p
for some
inner automorphism
\iota
of
G
. Otherwise
we apply Lemma
5.7
, noting that
the image of every
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}^{j})

is contained in a conjugate of
H
by Lemma
3.7
.
∎

We now consider the following situation.
Let
\Gamma
be a retractable centered splitting of

G
,
and let
B
be a bottom vertex group.
Given a surface subgroup
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\subset B
, and a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
, we wish to construct a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p^{\prime}
with values in
B
.

Figure 12:
Example
5.10
: there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\rightarrow G
, but none from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})
to
B
.

The following simple example shows that additional assumptions are required.

Example 5.10

(see Figure
12
)
.

As in Example
4.7
, we consider a group
B=B_{1}*_{g=[x_{B},y_{B}]}F(x_{B},y_{B})
obtained by attaching a punctured torus
\Sigma_{B}
to a group
B_{1}
, but we now require that
g
is
not
a commutator in
B_{1}
. Construct
G=B*_{g=[x,y]}F(x,y)
by attaching a second punctured torus
\Sigma
to
B_{1}
in the same way as
\Sigma_{B}
.
There is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\to G
with image
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, but no non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\to B
since
g
is not a commutator in
B_{1}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\Gamma
be a retractable centered splitting of
G
,
with central vertex group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
,
and let

B
be a bottom vertex group.
Let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})<B
be a surface subgroup, and let

p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\to G
be a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map.

If
p
satisfies one of the following conditions, there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\to B
:

1.

no conjugate of the image of
p
contains a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
;

2.

p
is pinching.

Moreover,
p^{\prime}
is pinching or
p^{\prime}=\iota\circ p
with
\iota
an inner automorphism of
G
.

Proof.

Assume 1.
If
p
does not pinch, then
it
takes values in a conjugate of
B
by Lemma
4.11
,
so we can take
p^{\prime}=\iota\circ p
for some inner automorphism
\iota
.
If
p
is pinching,
consider a maximal collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of pinched curves on
\Sigma_{B}
.
Lemma
4.11
ensures that the fundamental group of every connected component
of
\Sigma_{B}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}

is mapped into a conjugate of a bottom group of
\Gamma
.
One can then apply Lemma
5.7
:
if such a component contains a component of
\partial\Sigma_{B}
, Remark
4.4

implies that this bottom group has to be
B
since
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{B})\subset B
and
p
agrees with conjugations
on the boundary subgroups.

If 1 does not hold but
p
is pinching, we define
\hat{p}=\rho\circ p
, with
\rho:G\to\tilde{B}_{1}*\dots*\tilde{B}_{n}
a retraction associated to
\Gamma

provided by Proposition
4.21
. The image of
\hat{p}
is non-abelian because it is CSA and
contains a finite index subgroup of the non-abelian group
\rho(\pi_{1}(\Sigma))
,
so
\hat{p}
is a pinching non-degenerate boundary-preserving map with values in
\tilde{B}_{1}*\dots*\tilde{B}_{n}
.
The group
B
is conjugate to some
\tilde{B}_{i}
, and
we apply Lemma

5.9
to find
p^{\prime}
with values in

B
.
∎

5.3
Retracting a Grushko factor

In this subsection we introduce a blowup of a centered splitting that will enable us to understand how a freely decomposable group may be an étage of surface type. In particular, we will show that, if
G
is an extended étage of surface type, so is one of its Grushko factors.
As a corollary, we will see that a free group has no retractable splitting (this is a main step in the proof
of homogeneity of free groups
[
PS12
]
).

Figure 13:
The Grushko blowup
\Lambda
of
\Gamma
,
and the resulting centered splitting
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
with fundamental group
G_{1}
and base
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

Definition 5.12

(Grushko blowup of a centered splitting, see Figure
13
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
with bottom vertex groups
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
and central group
Q
.
The
Grushko blowup
of
\Gamma
is the splitting
\Lambda
obtained by
blowing up every bottom vertex of
\Gamma
using a Grushko decomposition
\Lambda_{i}
of
B_{i}
relative to the incident edge groups (see Subsection
3.4
).

We denote
by
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\Lambda
the union of all edges carrying a non-trivial group
(the star of the central vertex).
The fundamental group of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a free factor
G_{1}
of
G
containing
Q
.

The definition is illustrated in Figure
13
,
which depicts a case where there are two bottom vertex groups
B_{1},B_{2}
.
If, for
some
i
, the group
B_{i}
is freely decomposable relative to the incident edge groups, we choose a relative Grushko decomposition

\Lambda_{i}
with a central vertex
w_{i}
carrying a trivial group joined by a single edge to each vertex with non-trivial group.
Some of these vertices (carrying
\hat{B}_{1}
,
\hat{B}_{2}
,
\hat{B}_{3}
on the figure) have at least one edge of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
attached to them,
the base
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the free product of their groups. The other vertex groups of the
\Lambda_{i}
’s (
G_{2}
,…,
G_{4}
on the figure) are Grushko factors of
G
. There may also be loops attached at
w_{i}
, corresponding to the free group in the relative Grushko
decomposition of
B_{i}
. The edges with trivial group induce a decomposition of the form
G=G_{1}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}*F

with
G_{1}=\pi_{1}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}})
and
F
free (of rank 4 on Figure
13
).

Note that the splitting
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
fails to be
minimal if,
for some
i
,
an edge
e
of
\Gamma
containing
v_{i}
carries a group
A_{e}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
B_{i}
is a free product
B_{i}=A_{e}*B^{\prime}_{i}
, and the groups carried by all other edges of
\Gamma
containing
v_{i}
are contained (up to conjugacy) in the complementary free factor
B^{\prime}_{i}
.
If this does not happen,
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is minimal and is a centered splitting of
G_{1}
.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
, with central vertex
Q
and base
B
.
If
\Gamma
is retractable, then:

1.

the group
G_{1}=\pi_{1}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}})
is a Grushko factor of
G
containing
Q

(i.e.
G_{1}
is one-ended);

2.

\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is minimal, and is a retractable centered splitting of
G_{1}
;

3.

there is a Grushko decomposition
G=G_{1}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}*F

such that

B\simeq B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}*F^{\prime},

with
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
the base of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}

(possibly not one-ended) and

F^{\prime}
a free factor of the free group
F
.

Remark 5.14
.

By Assertion 1, collapsing all edges of
\Lambda
with non-trivial stabilizer yields a Grushko decomposition of
G
.

We derive several consequences of the proposition before proving it, starting with two obvious corollaries.

Corollary 5.15
.

If
G
has a retractable splitting, so does one of its Grushko factors. ∎

Corollary 5.16

(
[
Per11
,
PS12
]
)
.

A free group is not an extended étage of surface type.
∎

If
G
has a Grushko decomposition
G=G_{1}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}*F
, define
r(G)
as
G_{1}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}
.

Corollary 5.17
.

If
G
is an extended tower over
B
, then

r(G)
is an extended tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
r(B)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the result for an étage. It is clear if
G=B*{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
B
, Proposition
5.13
says that
G_{1}
is an extended étage over
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
,
so by Remark
4.30

r(G)=G_{1}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}
is
an extended étage of surface type over
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}
, which is of the form
r(B)*F^{\prime\prime}
with
F^{\prime\prime}
free.

∎

The following result is a partial converse to Proposition
5.2
.

Corollary 5.18
.

If
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is an
extended tower over a group
B
for some
r\geq 1
,
then
G
is an extended
tower over a group
B^{\prime}
such that
B\simeq B^{\prime}*{\mathbb{F}}_{s}
for some
s\geq 0
. ∎

Proof.

By the previous corollary,
r(G)=r(G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r})
is an extended tower over
B^{\prime}:=r(B)
, and
G
is an extended tower over
r(G)
.
∎

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.13
.
The key step is the following fact.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
. If it is retractable,
the group
G_{1}
of Definition
5.12
is one-ended.

Proof of Proposition
5.13
from Proposition
5.19
.

Since

G_{1}
is one-ended,
it is a Grushko factor of
G
.

Minimality of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
will be proved in Lemma
5.20
. Since
\Gamma
is retractable, there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map from
Q
to
G
. By Lemma
5.9
there is one with values in
G_{1}
, so
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is a retractable splitting of
G_{1}
.

The structure of
B
follows from the above description of the Grushko blowup;
the rank of
F^{\prime}
is the sum of the first Betti numbers of the graphs
\Lambda_{i}
(the total number of loops at the vertices
w_{i}
); in the rank of
F
there is
an extra term equal to the number of bottom groups of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
minus the number of bottom groups of
\Gamma
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
5.19
. This
requires several lemmas.

Lemma 5.20
.

If a centered splitting
\Gamma
of
G
is retractable,
the splitting
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
of Definition
5.12
is minimal.

Proof.

We assume that
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is not minimal and we argue towards a contradiction.
As explained above, there is an edge
e=vv_{i}
of
\Gamma

carrying a group
A_{e}
such that
B_{i}=A_{e}*B^{\prime}_{i}
, and the groups carried by all other edges of
\Gamma
containing
v_{i}
are contained (up to conjugacy) in
B^{\prime}_{i}
.

Let
C
be the boundary component of
\Sigma
associated to the edge
e
.
Fixing a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p
, we let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a maximal family of pinched curves on
\Sigma
, and we consider the component

\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{C}}
containing
C
. By Lemma
4.9
(and Remark
4.10
)

p
maps
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
into
B_{i}
(up to conjugacy).
Moreover,
\pi_{1}(C)
is mapped surjectively onto a conjugate of
A_{e}
,
and if
C^{\prime}
is another component of
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\partial\Sigma
then
\pi_{1}(C^{\prime})
is mapped into a conjugate of
B^{\prime}_{i}
. Projecting
B_{i}
to

A_{e}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
by killing
B^{\prime}_{i}
, we
deduce that a generator of
{\mathbb{Z}}
is a product of commutators or squares (depending on whether
\Sigma^{\prime}
is orientable or not), a contradiction.
∎

The following remark will be used in Subsections
6.2.1
and
9.2
.

Remark 5.21
.

Assume that a cyclic splitting
\Gamma
has a vertex
v
which is as in Definition
3.5
(surface-type vertices), except that the map from the set of incident edges to the set of boundary components of
\Sigma
is injective but not surjective; in other words,
\Sigma
has at least one “free” boundary component
(this may occur
at a flexible vertex of a JSJ decomposition relative to a cyclic subgroup
A
,
with a free boundary component
whose
fundamental group
contains a conjugate of
A
).
The argument used to prove the lemma then says that there is no non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow G
.
In particular, if
\Gamma
is a centered splitting which is not minimal (thus contradicting our standing assumption), it is not retractable.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
with central vertex group
Q
.

If
J
is a finitely generated one-ended subgroup of
G
, then some conjugate of
J
either is contained in one of the bottom vertex groups,
or contains a finite index subgroup of
Q
.

Proof.

If
J
is elliptic in the Bass-Serre tree
T
of
\Gamma
, it is contained (up to conjugacy) in a bottom group
B_{i}
(not in
Q
, which is a free group). If not, the action of
J
on its minimal subtree defines a non-trivial splitting
\Gamma_{J}
. Using conjugations, we may assume that
\Gamma_{J}
has a vertex group
J\cap Q
, with an incident edge group of the form
J\cap C
where
C
is a maximal boundary subgroup of
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Since
J
is one-ended,
J\cap C
is non-trivial. It is maximal cyclic in
J\cap Q

because
C
is maximal cyclic in
Q
.
By 1-acylindricity of
T
and minimality of
\Gamma_{J}
, we have
J\cap C\neq J\cap Q
. Lemma
3.6
then implies that
J\cap Q
has finite index in
Q
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

If
J
is not elliptic in
T
, the proof shows that
J
has a cyclic splitting with a surface-type vertex whose associated surface is a finite cover of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 5.24
.

Let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
.
If each bottom group
B_{i}
is one-ended
relative to the incident edge groups,
then
G
is one-ended.

Remark 5.25
.

In this lemma, the implicit assumption that
\Gamma
is a minimal splitting is essential.

Proof.

By
[
Hor17
, Lemma 6.11]
,
if all Grushko factors of
G
are elliptic in
\Gamma
, then some vertex group of
\Gamma
is freely decomposable
relative to its incident edge groups,
contradicting our assumption.
Thus, there is a Grushko factor
J
of
G
which is not elliptic in
\Gamma
. Lemma
5.22
implies that (up to conjugacy)
J
contains a finite index subgroup of
Q
, hence contains
Q
.
This easily implies that
G
is one-ended.

Indeed,
Q
is elliptic in any
G
-tree
S
with trivial edge stabilizers, and so are edge groups of
\Gamma
, as well as the groups
B_{i}
because of the assumption of the lemma. Since edge stabilizers of
S
are trivial, adjacent vertex stabilizers of the Bass-Serre tree of
\Gamma
must fix the same vertex of
S
, so
S
must be trivial.
∎

Proposition
5.19
follows by applying Lemma
5.24
to the splitting
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
of
G_{1}
, which is minimal by Lemma
5.20
.

5.4
Reading an étage in the JSJ

In this subsection it is important to assume that
G
is a CSA group. We also assume that
G
is one-ended. It then has a
canonical cyclic JSJ splitting
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
(associated to
the tree
(T_{a})^{*}_{c}
of Theorem 9.5 of
[
GL17
]
, see Subsection
3.6
and
[
GL17
]
).

Now let
\Gamma
be a centered splitting of
G
, with central vertex
v
and central vertex group
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. The main result of this subsection is Lemma
5.29
, saying that
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
is retractable if
\Gamma
is retractable (provided that
G
is not a closed surface group).
Recall (Definition
4.23
) that a splitting is retractable if one of its non-exceptional surface-type vertex groups has a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map.

We first explain how to obtain
\Gamma
from
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
(see Figure
14
).
Because
G
is one-ended, the central vertex group
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is elliptic in
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
(see Theorem 5.27 of
[
GL17
]
). It is contained (up to conjugacy) in a vertex group
G_{w}
of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
which is neither rigid
nor abelian, so is surface-type. Let
S
be the associated surface, so that
G_{w}=\pi_{1}(S)
.

Figure 14:
The surface
\Sigma
of the centered splitting
\Gamma
(pictured on the left) is contained in the surface
S
of the JSJ splitting
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
(on the right).

The splitting
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
is universally compatible (see
[
GL17
]
): there is a splitting
\hat{\Gamma}
which collapses onto both
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
. We may assume that
\hat{\Gamma}

is the lcm of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
:
no edge is collapsed in both (see
[
GL17
]
, Section A.5). The group
Q
is elliptic in
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
, hence contained in a vertex group
G_{\hat{v}}
of
\hat{\Gamma}
. We claim that edges of
\hat{\Gamma}
incident on
\hat{v}
are not collapsed in
\Gamma
.

To prove the claim, we work with the Bass-Serre trees and we denote by
\pi:T_{\hat{\Gamma}}\rightarrow T_{\Gamma}

the collapse map. We view
\hat{v},v
as vertices of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}},T_{\Gamma}
, with
\pi(\hat{v})=v
.
Consider the subtree
Y=\pi^{-1}(v)
of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the set of edges
e=v_{e}w_{e}

of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}
with
v_{e}\in Y
and
w_{e}\notin Y
which are not collapsed by
\pi
(it may be identified with the star of
v
in
T_{\Gamma}
).
By minimality of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}
,
Y
is the convex hull of
\{v_{e}\mid e\in{\mathcal{E}}\}
.

Note that
G_{e}
fixes the segment
[\hat{v},v_{e}]
for
e\in{\mathcal{E}}
.
Since distinct edges of
T_{\Gamma}
incident on
v
have non-commuting stabilizers,
this is also the case for any pair of distinct edges
e,e^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}
, so

e,e^{\prime}
cannot be in the same connected component of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}\setminus\{\hat{v}\}
.
If
e\in{\mathcal{E}}
and
v_{e}\neq\hat{v}
, minimality of
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}
implies that
there is no edge incident on the open segment
(\hat{v},v_{e})
,
and
e
is the only edge incident on
v_{e}
not contained in
[\hat{v},v_{e}]
.
Since there is no redundant vertex in
T_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, it follows that
v_{e}=\hat{v}
for all
e\in{\mathcal{E}}
, so
Y=\{\hat{v}\}
. This proves the claim: edges of
\hat{\Gamma}
incident on
\hat{v}
are not collapsed in
\Gamma
.

On the other hand, the preimage of
w
in
\hat{\Gamma}
is a graph of groups decomposition of
\pi_{1}(S)
, and the minimal subgraph of groups is dual to a family of disjoint simple closed curves on
S
by a standard result (
[
MS84
, Theorem III.2.6]
). The group
Q
is a vertex group of this splitting (it is carried by
\hat{v}
), so
\Sigma
may be identified with a subsurface of
S
(see Figure
14
). In particular,
S
is non-exceptional if
\Sigma
is non-exceptional (unless
G=\pi_{1}(N_{3})
).

To sum up:

Lemma 5.26
.

One may obtain any centered splitting

\Gamma
from
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
as follows: one selects a surface-type vertex
w
of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
and an incompressible subsurface
\Sigma
of the associated surface
S
, one refines
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
by blowing up
w
using a splitting of
G_{w}=\pi_{1}(S)
having a vertex
v
carrying
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, and one collapses all edges of the refined splitting not containing
v
. ∎

Since the set of incompressible surfaces of a given surface
S
is finite up to the action of the modular group of
S
, we obtain the following finiteness result.

Corollary 5.27
.

Let
G
be a one-ended torsion-free CSA group. Up to isomorphism, the set of groups
B
such that
G
has a centered splitting with base isomorphic to
B
is finite. ∎

Keeping the same notations as in Lemma
5.26
, we now compare the base
B_{\Gamma}
of the centered splitting
\Gamma
to
B_{S}
,
the base of the centered splitting obtained from
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
by collapsing all edges not containing
w
.

Lemma 5.28
.

B_{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to the free product of
B_{S}
with a free group.

Proof.

See Figure
14
.
View
\hat{\Gamma}
as a graph of spaces
X
, so that
B_{\Gamma}
(resp.
B_{S}
) is the free product of the fundamental groups
of the connected components of
X\setminus\Sigma
(resp.
X\setminus S
).
Each connected component of
X\setminus\Sigma
is a union of connected components of
X\setminus S
,
attached by surfaces with boundary. Since the closure of each component of
S\setminus\Sigma
contains a component of
\partial\Sigma
,
the fundamental group of each connected component of
X\setminus\Sigma
is the free product of the fundamental groups
of the connected components of
X\setminus S
which it contains, and a free group.
∎

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
G
be a one-ended torsion-free CSA group, not a closed surface group. If
G
has a retractable cyclic splitting
\Gamma
, then the canonical cyclic JSJ splitting

\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
of
G

is retractable.

Proof.

We may assume that
\Gamma
is a centered splitting of
G
, with central vertex group
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. Let
w
and
S
be as in Lemma
5.26
.

Let
p:Q\to G
be a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map associated to
\Gamma
, and
let
\hat{p}:G\to G
be an extension of
p
“by the identity” as in Lemma
3.8
(viewing
Q
as a vertex group of
\Gamma
). Its restriction to
\pi_{1}(S)
is a boundary-preserving map, because
any maximal boundary subgroup of
S
(i.e. an incident edge group near the vertex
w
of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
) is an incident edge group of
Q
or is contained in
a conjugate of a bottom vertex group of
\Gamma

(because edges of
\hat{\Gamma}
containing
\hat{v}
are not collapsed in
\Gamma
).

We show that the boundary-preserving map
\hat{p}_{|\pi_{1}(S)}

is non-degenerate. Clearly its image is non-abelian.
If
\hat{p}
sends
\pi_{1}(S)
isomorphically onto a conjugate, then
p
is non-pinching, so Lemma
4.13
implies that

\Gamma
has a single bottom vertex group
B_{1}
,
and that
\hat{p}(G)
is contained in a conjugate of
B_{1}
.
In particular, the image of
\pi_{1}(S)
contains no conjugate of
Q
, a contradiction.
∎

6
Towers as an ordering: core and prototypes

In this section
G
is a CSA group. We will define prototypes, and associate a core
\mathrm{c}(G)
to
G
. We will see in Section
7
that, when
G
is a non-abelian hyperbolic group, it is elementarily equivalent to
\mathrm{c}(G)
or
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, and two non-abelian hyperbolic groups are elementarily equivalent if and only if their cores are isomorphic.

6.1
Defining an order

Let
G
be a torsion-free CSA group.

Recall from Subsection
4.5
that a centered splitting
\Gamma
is retractable if there is a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map; equivalently (see Proposition
4.21
) there is a retraction
\rho
from
G
to a product of conjugates
\tilde{B}_{i}
of the
B_{i}
’s (to
\tilde{B}_{1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
if
\Gamma
is simple and
B_{1}
is abelian) with
\rho(Q)
non-abelian.
We then say that
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
H=\tilde{B}_{1}*\dots*\tilde{B}_{n}
.

We shall use extended étages to define an order on the set of isomorphism classes of CSA groups.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
G,H
be
torsion-free CSA groups.

We define
G\mathrel{>_{1}}H
if

G
is an extended étage over
H
.
In other words,
G>_{1}H
if
G\simeq H*{\mathbb{Z}}
, or
G
has a retractable splitting with base isomorphic to
H
.

We then define

G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
if
G
is an extended tower over
H
, i.e. if
there is a sequence
G=G_{0},G_{1},\dots,G_{p}\simeq H
, with
p\geq 0
, such that
G_{i}\mathrel{>_{1}}G_{i+1}
for each
i
. We write
G\mathrel{>_{t}}H
if
p>0
.

In particular, the free group
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
satisfies
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\{1\}
.

Remark 6.2
.

If
G\mathrel{>_{t}}H
, then
H

may be viewed as a proper quotient of
G
, and also as a subgroup of
G
.
If
G
is hyperbolic,
H
is quasiconvex, so is a hyperbolic group.

Also note that
G*G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H*H^{\prime}
if
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
and
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H^{\prime}
, and
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G
.

Lemma 6.3
.

G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
if and only if there is a sequence
G=G_{0},G_{1},\dots,G_{p}
such that
G_{i}
is an
étage of surface type over
G_{i+1}
, and
G_{p}\simeq H*F
with
F
free (possibly trivial or cyclic).

If
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
with
H
non-abelian, there exists a free group
F
such that
G*F
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
H
.
The converse is true if
H

has no cyclic free factor.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from Remark
4.31
. The second one follows from
Corollary
5.4
, and its converse from Corollary
5.18
.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

If we restrict to non-abelian CSA groups with no
cyclic free factor,
then
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
if and only if some
G*F
with
F
free is a simple tower over
H
.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

The relation
\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}
defines a partial order on the set of isomorphism classes of
torsion-free CSA groups. There is no infinite sequence
G=G_{0}\mathrel{>_{t}}G_{1}\mathrel{>_{t}}\cdots\mathrel{>_{t}}G_{i}\mathrel{>_{t}}\cdots
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.34
:
if
G\mathrel{>_{t}}H
, then
H
has smaller first Betti number mod 2.
When
G
is hyperbolic, one may also argue
that the
G_{i}
’s are
G
-limit groups,
and there is no infinite sequence of
G
-limit groups
G_{i}
such that
G_{i+1}
is a proper quotient of
G_{i}

[
Sel09
]
.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Given a torsion-free CSA group
G
, the set of groups
H

such that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
is finite up to isomorphism.

Proof.

By Lemma

6.3
and Proposition
6.5
,
it suffices to prove finiteness for the set of groups
H
such that
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
H
.

The result follows from Corollary
5.27
if
G
is one-ended.
If
G
is infinitely-ended, Proposition
5.13
expresses
H
as
H=B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}*G_{2}*\cdots*G_{k}*F^{\prime}
,
where
G=G_{1}*G_{2}*\cdots*G_{k}*F
is a Grushko decomposition of
G
, the group
F^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a free factor of
F
,
and
G_{1}
is an extended étage of surface type over
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Finiteness follows from the one-ended case.
∎

Proposition
6.6
will imply that, given a torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
, there are only finitely many
isomorphism classes of groups that elementarily embed in
G
(Corollary
8.4
).

Definition 6.7

(Prototype)
.

A torsion-free CSA group
G
is a
prototype
if it is minimal for
\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}
.

The trivial group, and groups with no cyclic splitting, in particular groups with Kazhdan’s property (T), are prototypes.
On the other hand,
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is not a prototype.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
G
be a
torsion-free CSA group.
The following are equivalent:

1.

G
is a prototype;

2.

G
has no cyclic free factor and is not an extended étage of surface type (i.e. it
has no retractable splitting);

3.

G
is a free product of one-ended groups which are not extended étages of surface type.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
is a prototype. By minimality, it cannot be written
G=H*{\mathbb{Z}}
and cannot be an extended étage of surface type.
This proves
1\Rightarrow 2
.

For
2\Rightarrow 3
, we simply note that, if
G
is not an extended étage, neither are its Grushko factors by Remark
4.30
.

If
G
is a free product of one-ended groups which are not extended étages of surface type, it has no cyclic free factor, and is not an extended étage of surface type by Corollary
5.15
. It is minimal for the order, hence is a prototype.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

In particular, if
G
is not a prototype, it has a cyclic free factor or one of its Grushko factors is an extended étage (necessarily of surface type).

A one-ended group is a prototype if and only if it is not an extended étage (of surface type).
A
group
is a prototype if and only if it is a free product of one-ended prototypes.

If
G
is a prototype, no
G*F
with
F
free is an extended étage of surface type (this follows from Corollary
5.17
).

The fundamental group of a closed hyperbolic surface
S
is a prototype if and only
S
is exceptional (the non-orientable surface of genus 3), see Example
4.35
.

The following theorem was proved by Sela
[
Sel09
]
for hyperbolic groups, using their classification
up to elementary equivalence.
We will give a proof independent of this theory.

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
G
be a
torsion-free CSA group. Up to isomorphism, there exists a unique prototype
\mathrm{c}(G)
such that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\mathrm{c}(G)
.

Definition 6.11
.

\mathrm{c}(G)
is called the
core
of
G
.

Remark 6.12
.

For instance
\mathrm{c}(G)\simeq G
if
G
is a prototype, and
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
if
G
is a free group or a non-exceptional closed surface group. The core of
G
embeds into
G
as a retract,
but not in a canonical way in general (see Subsection
10.4
).
Also note that
\mathrm{c}(G_{1}*G_{2})\simeq\mathrm{c}(G_{1})*\mathrm{c}(G_{2})
by Remark
4.30
.

Remark 6.13
.

The equality

\mathrm{c}(G_{1}*G_{2})\simeq\mathrm{c}(G_{1})*\mathrm{c}(G_{2})
requires

\mathrm{c}({\mathbb{F}}_{n})
to be trivial.
This has a drawback
when considering elementary equivalence:
we will see in Section

7
that a hyperbolic group
G
is equivalent to
c(G)
if
c(G)\neq\{1\}
,
to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
if
G
is non-abelian with
c(G)=\{1\}
. We will
therefore introduce (Definition
7.6
)
the
elementary core

\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
, which for
G

a non-abelian hyperbolic group is always elementarily equivalent to
G
(in particular,
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}({\mathbb{F}}_{2})={\mathbb{F}}_{2}
), but
at the cost of losing the nice behaviour under free products.

The existence of
\mathrm{c}(G)
in Theorem
6.10
follows directly from the descending chain condition (Proposition
6.5
).
Our proof of uniqueness relies on
the next lemma, which will be proved in Subsection
6.2
. When
G
is hyperbolic, uniqueness may also be deduced from the classification up to elementary equivalence (see
[
Sel09
]
).

We denote by

{\mathcal{G}}
the class of all
torsion-free CSA groups.

Lemma 6.14

(Minimal base)
.

Given
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
, there exists
M\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
and, if
G\mathrel{>_{1}}G^{\prime}
with
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
then
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
.

The group
M
will be constructed in Subsection
6.2.2
from the minimal base of a splitting, introduced in Subsection
6.2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
6.10
from Lemma
6.14
.

Noetherianity (Proposition
6.5
) guarantees the existence of a prototype
P
such that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}P
.
We show that confluence holds for
G
: if
G\mathrel{>_{t}}G_{1}
and
G\mathrel{>_{t}}G_{2}
, there exists
G^{\prime}
such that
G_{1}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G^{\prime}
and
G_{2}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G^{\prime}
. This clearly implies uniqueness of
P
, which is what we need to prove.

By noetherianity, we may assume that confluence holds for all
\tilde{G}
such that
G\mathrel{>_{t}}\tilde{G}
.
Since
G\mathrel{>_{t}}G_{i}
, there exists
A_{i}
such that
G\mathrel{>_{1}}A_{i}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G_{i}
. The group
M
provided by Lemma
6.14
satisfies
A_{i}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
for
i=1,2
. We assume that confluence holds for
A_{i}
, so there exists
C_{i}
such that
G_{i}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}C_{i}
and
M\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}C_{i}
. As confluence holds for
M
, there exists
G^{\prime}
such that

C_{i}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G^{\prime}
, hence
G_{i}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G^{\prime}
.
∎

6.2
Proof of the Minimal Base Lemma
6.14

6.2.1
The minimal base of a splitting

We fix a non-trivial cyclic splitting
\Gamma
of
G
.
We assume that its Bass-Serre tree is 2-acylindrical, and no edge joins two surface-type vertices or two
abelian vertices.
We will apply this subsection to the canonical cyclic JSJ splitting
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
of a one-ended CSA group, but the CSA property is not really necessary here.

Recall (Definition
4.23
) that a non-exceptional surface-type vertex group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of
\Gamma

is retractable (in
G
) if there exists a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
. By definition,
p
acts as a conjugation on each boundary subgroup, has non-abelian image, and is not an isomorphism onto a conjugate of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.

Figure 15:
Twinnings between surfaces of a splitting

As in Example
5.10
, a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
may be an isomorphism onto (a conjugate of) some
other
surface-type vertex group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
of
\Gamma
. We then say that
p
is a
twinning
of
\Sigma
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a
twin
of
\Sigma
(see Figure
15
). On the other hand, if
p
is not a twinning,
we say that
it is a
strong boundary-preserving map

and that

\Sigma
, or
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, is
strongly retractable
.

Let
p
be a twinning. Boundary subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
are mapped to conjugates, hence to boundary subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. This implies that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
have the same number of boundary components and are homeomorphic (see Lemma 3.13 of
[
Per11
]
).
In particular,
p^{-1}
is a twinning.
Twinning thus induces an equivalence relation on the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}
consisting of all surfaces associated to surface-type vertices
of
\Gamma
(by convention,
\Sigma
is twinned with itself). This relation is compatible with retractability and strong retractability.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be any subset of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}
. If we remove from
\Gamma
the open stars of all vertices associated to surfaces not
in
{\mathcal{S}}
, we get a graph
\Gamma({\mathcal{S}})
whose components we denote by
\Gamma_{\!i}(S)
.

The graphs of groups
\Gamma_{\!i}(S)
may be points. They are not necessarily minimal: they may have terminal cyclic vertices
v
with the incident edge group equal to the vertex group.
If this happens, we remove
v
and the open edge (this does not change the fundamental group of the graph of groups). If the edge joins
v
to a surface-type vertex
w
, this vertex is no longer surface-type because the
surface has gained a free boundary component; this will not be a problem because by Remark
5.21

w
cannot be retractable as a vertex of
\Gamma_{\!i}(S)
(see the proof of Proposition
6.17
below).

From now on, we shall denote by
\Gamma_{\!i}(S)
the minimal graph of groups obtained by removing edges as just explained (it may consist of a single vertex, cyclic or not).
Generalizing Definition
4.1
, we denote by
B_{i}({\mathcal{S}})
the fundamental group
of

\Gamma_{\!i}(S)
, and by
B({\mathcal{S}})
the free product of the groups
B_{i}({\mathcal{S}})
, which we call the
base
of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Given
\Gamma
,
let
M(\Gamma)
be the base
of a subset
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

it contains all non-retractable surfaces;

•

it contains no strongly retractable surface;

•

if
\Sigma
is retractable but not strongly retractable,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
contains exactly one surface in the twinning equivalence class of
\Sigma
(which cannot be a singleton).

The next lemma says that
M(\Gamma)
is well-defined up to isomorphism, we call it the
minimal base
of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Up to isomorphism,
M(\Gamma)
is independent of the choice of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
: it does not change if we replace a surface
\Sigma
which is retractable but not strongly retractable by a twin surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be the vertices of
\Gamma
carrying
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
respectively.
By assumption, there is an isomorphism
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})

which acts as a conjugation on each boundary subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
If
C
is an incident edge group at
v
, its image is an incident edge group at
v^{\prime}
, and by 2-acylindricity the edges of
\Gamma
carrying
C
and
p(C)
join
v
and
v^{\prime}
to the same vertex.
This implies that there is an automorphism of
\Gamma
(viewed as a graph with no extra structure) sending
v
to
v^{\prime}
and fixing all other vertices.

The twinning isomorphism
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and its inverse
act by conjugations on the boundary subgroups
of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
They may therefore be extended to an automorphism of
G

“by the identity” as in Lemma
3.8
.

This may also be viewed topologically.
The group
G
is
the fundamental group of a graph of spaces containing
two homeomorphic surfaces
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
attached to the rest of the space with the same attaching maps.
The twinning isomorphism is
represented by a pair of inverse homeomorphisms between
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
which fix the boundary. These homeomorphisms
may be extended by the identity to the rest of the space.
This yields an automorphism of
G
exchanging
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})

and acting as a conjugation on each

B_{i}({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\})
.
∎

If
S
is a retractable surface of
\Gamma
, we write

{\mathcal{S}}_{-S}
for
{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\{S\}}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
S
and
\Sigma
be distinct surfaces of a splitting
\Gamma
.
Let
\Gamma_{\!i}({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
be the component of
\Gamma({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
containing the vertex carrying
\Sigma
, and
B_{i}({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
its fundamental group.

If
S
is retractable and
\Sigma
is strongly retractable (in
G
), then
\Sigma
remains strongly retractable in
B_{i}({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
.

Proof.

Let
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
be a strong boundary-preserving map. If it satisfies one of the conditions of Lemma
5.11
, we get a strong boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to B_{i}({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
as required.

We may therefore assume that
p
is non-pinching, and by Lemma
4.11
that
\Sigma
has an incompressible subsurface
Z
whose fundamental group is mapped to
a finite index subgroup
K
of
\pi_{1}(S)
.

Since
S
is retractable,
G
is an extended étage over
B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
and there is a retraction
\rho
from
G
to a subgroup
\tilde{B}
isomorphic
to
B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
(see Proposition
4.21
). As in the proof of Lemma
5.11
, we define
p^{\prime}=\rho\circ p

and we apply Lemma
5.9

to get retractability in
B_{i}({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
rather than just in
\tilde{B}
.
We only have to make sure that
p^{\prime}
is not an isomorphism between
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and a conjugate of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})

for some surface
\Sigma^{\prime}\neq S
(possibly equal to
\Sigma
).

If it is,
\rho
is injective on
K
, hence on
\pi_{1}(S)
because
G
is torsion-free.
Since
\rho(K)\subset\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and
\rho(\pi_{1}(S))
contains
\rho(K)
with finite index,
it follows that
\rho(\pi_{1}(S))\subset\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
,
because no subgroup of
G
contains
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
as a proper subgroup of finite index.
Boundary subgroups of
\pi_{1}(S)
are mapped to conjugates, hence into boundary subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
.
Lemma 3.10 of
[
Per11
]
implies that the image of
\pi_{1}(S)
has finite index in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. Using the same complexity
k
as in Lemma 3.12 of
[
Per11
]
, we now write
k(\Sigma)\geq k(Z)\geq k(S)\geq k(\Sigma^{\prime})=k(\Sigma)
. All these complexities are equal, so
Z=\Sigma
and
p
is an isomorphism between
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and
\pi_{1}(S)
, hence not a strong boundary-preserving map.
∎

Proposition 6.17
.

G
is an extended tower over
M(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

If
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, it is retractable and
G
is an extended étage over
B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
. Retracting surfaces iteratively
will then express
B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
as an extended étage over some
B({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\{S,S^{\prime}\})
,
and eventually

G
as an extended tower over
M(\Gamma)=B({\mathcal{S}}_{0})
. We just have to be careful, as the retractability type of surfaces might change during the process.

First suppose that there is a surface
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
which is retractable (but not strongly retractable). Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
be its set of twins (excluding
S
itself). We may retract the surfaces of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
using the twinnings with
S
, so as to express
G
as an extended tower over
B({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{1})
. Note that
\Gamma({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{1})
is connected, because the vertices carrying
S
and its twins are adjacent to the same
vertices.

By Lemma
6.16
, surfaces not in
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
remain
retractable in
\Gamma({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{1})

(because they have a twin or they are strongly retractable). The surface
S
itself either remains a surface but becomes non-retractable, or it stops being a surface (it gains a free boundary component when we make
\Gamma({\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{1})
minimal, see Remark
5.21
).

Performing this operation for every retractable
surface
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
lets us assume that every retractable surface is strongly retractable. Using Lemma
6.16
again, we can now retract them one by one.
∎

6.2.2
Proof of confluence

We can now prove Lemma
6.14
.
We first assume that
G
(a CSA group) is one-ended. Let
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
be its canonical cyclic JSJ decomposition (see Subsection
3.6
). It satisfies the assumptions of Subsection
6.2.1
.

If
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}

is non-trivial,
we define

M=M(\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}})

as the minimal base of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
.
We have
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
by Proposition
6.17
. We now suppose that
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
G^{\prime}
and we show
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
.

If the étage is associated to a surface
S
of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
, this surface is retractable; we can choose
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
with
S\notin{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, and we have seen in the proof of Proposition
6.17
that
G^{\prime}=B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
is an extended tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
M
.
In general, Lemmas
5.26
and
5.28
yield a surface
S
of
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
such that
G^{\prime}
is the free product of a free group with
B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})
, so
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}B({\mathcal{S}}_{-S})\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
.

Still assuming
G
one-ended, suppose that
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}

is trivial. There are two cases. If
G
is either rigid (it has no cyclic splitting) or
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
or the exceptional closed surface group, then
G
has no retractable splitting,
and there is no
G^{\prime}
such that
G\mathrel{>_{1}}G^{\prime}
;
we define
M=G
in this case.
Otherwise,
G
is the fundamental group of a non-exceptional closed surface (see
[
GL17
]
).
As explained in Example
4.35
,
G
is an extended tower over

\{1\}
, and we define
M=\{1\}
.
Any
G^{\prime}
such that
G\mathrel{>_{1}}G^{\prime}
is free hence
\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\{1\}
.

The lemma is clear if
G
is cyclic, so suppose that
G
has infinitely many ends.
If
G
is free, we can take
M=\{1\}
.
Otherwise, we consider a Grushko decomposition
G=G_{1}*\cdots*G_{k}*F
with
F
free.
Since
G_{i}
is one-ended, it has a minimal base
M_{i}
and
we define
M
as the free product of the

M_{i}
’s.

Using Remark
6.2
, we can write

G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}G_{1}*\cdots*G_{k}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M_{1}*\cdots*M_{k}=M
. Similarly,
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M
if
G=G^{\prime}*{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
G
is an extended étage of surface type over
G^{\prime}
,
we get after reordering
G^{\prime}=B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}*G_{2}*\dots*G_{k}*F^{\prime}
as in Proposition
5.13
, with
G_{1}
an extended étage of surface type over
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We then have
B_{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M_{1}
by the one-ended case, hence
G^{\prime}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}M_{1}*\cdots*M_{k}
.

7
Elementary equivalence

Though motivated by model theory, the paper so far is purely group theory. We now give Sela’s classification of hyperbolic groups up to elementary equivalence.

From now on, we restrict to hyperbolic groups.
Recall that we associated a core
\mathrm{c}(G)
to any torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
(Definition
6.11
).

Theorem 7.1

(
[
Sel09
]
)
.

Two non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic groups are elementarily equivalent if and only if their cores are isomorphic.

We split this statement into two.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
G,H
be non-abelian
torsion-free hyperbolic groups. If
G
and
H
are
elementarily equivalent, their cores are isomorphic.

We shall give a proof of this theorem (the “only if” direction in Theorem
7.1
) in Section
9
(see Corollary

9.8
).
As we will see, the converse direction is an easy consequence of Theorem
2.1
(“Tarski”),
which contains the fact that

H*F\equiv H

if
F
is free and
H
is a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group, and in particular a solution to Tarski’s problem, so will
—unfortunately— not be proved here.
We recall its statement.

.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group. If
G
is a
simple

tower
over a non-abelian subgroup
H<G
, then the inclusion
H\hookrightarrow G
is an elementary embedding.
In particular,
G
and
H
are elementarily equivalent. ∎

It is important here that the
surfaces appearing in the tower be non-exceptional.
We shall also see (Theorem
10.13
)
that the conclusion may be wrong if
G
is a
non-simple
tower
over
H
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
G,H
be non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic groups.
If
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}H
, then
G
and
H
are
elementarily equivalent.

Proof.

Recall (Corollary
5.4
) that there exists
r\geq 0
such that
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over a subgroup
\tilde{H}

isomorphic to
H
.
Since
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over
G
(with étages of free product type), Theorem
2.1
implies

G\equiv G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}\equiv\tilde{H}\equiv H
.
∎

Example 7.4

(see Example
4.7
)
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group. Suppose that
g\in G
is non-trivial and is a commutator.
Then
\langle G,x,y\mid g=[x,y]\rangle
is elementarily equivalent to
G
.

Example 7.5

(see Example
4.35
)
.

The fundamental group of a closed surface
\Sigma
with
\chi(\Sigma)\leq-2

is elementarily equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

In order to prove the “if” direction in Theorem
7.1
,
we want to deduce from Theorem
2.1

that
G
is elementarily equivalent to its core
\mathrm{c}(G)
. Since the theorem does not apply when
H=\mathrm{c}(G)
is abelian (i.e. when
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
), we
introduce a slightly modified definition.

Recall that a group is a prototype if it is minimal for
\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}
, and the core
\mathrm{c}(G)
is the unique prototype such that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\mathrm{c}(G)
.
When
G
is a non-abelian free group,
\mathrm{c}(G)
is the trivial group, and in particular is not elementarily equivalent to
G
.
As we will see, the case where
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
is the only exception, and in this case
G
is elementarily equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
To overcome this exception, we give the following definition.

Definition 7.6

(Elementary core)
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group. We define its
elementary core

as the group
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
well-defined up to isomorphism by

\begin{cases}\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)=\mathrm{c}(G)&\text{if $\mathrm{c}(G)\neq 1$}\\
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)={\mathbb{F}}_{2}&\text{if $\mathrm{c}(G)=1$.}\\
\end{cases}

Note that
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
and
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(H)
are isomorphic if and only if
\mathrm{c}(G)
and
\mathrm{c}(H)
are isomorphic, so this change does not affect the validity of Theorems
7.1
and
7.2
. On the other hand, it is not always true that
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
(see Example
10.11
).
As noted in Remark
6.13
, although
\mathrm{c}(G_{1}*G_{2})=\mathrm{c}(G_{1})*\mathrm{c}(G_{2})
, this is no more true for
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}
.

We
can now state
the following consequence of “Tarski”, which, together with
Theorem
7.2
(proved in Subsection
9
), clearly implies Theorem
7.1
.

Corollary 7.7
.

Any non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
is
elementarily equivalent
to its elementary core
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
.

Proof.

This is clear if
\mathrm{c}(G)\neq\{1\}
using Corollary
7.3
, since
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\mathrm{c}(G)=\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
. If
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
, we have
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\{1\}
, hence
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
by Remark
4.30
, and
G\equiv G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\equiv{\mathbb{F}}_{2}=\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

Although
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
always embeds into
G
as a retract,
there are examples of hyperbolic groups for which
\mathrm{c}(G)
is non-trivial and does not embed elementarily in
G
(see Theorem
10.13
).

The following lemma will be used in Section
8
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group.
There exists
r\geq 0
such that
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
.

Proof.

By definition (see Subsection
6.1
),
G
is an extended tower over
\mathrm{c}(G)
.
If
\mathrm{c}(G)\neq\{1\}
, then Corollary
5.4
says that there exists
r\geq 0
such that

G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathrm{c}(G)=\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
.
If
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
, then
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
by Remark
4.30
, so there exists
r\geq 0
such that
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{2}*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}=\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
.
∎

It follows from Theorem
2.1

that many non-free finitely generated groups (in particular surface groups, see Example
7.5
) are elementarily equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. They may be characterized as follows.

Corollary 7.10

(Elementarily free groups)
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated non-abelian torsion-free group.
The following are equivalent:

1.

G
is elementarily equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
;

2.

G
is hyperbolic and
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)={\mathbb{F}}_{2}
;

3.

G
is hyperbolic and
\mathrm{c}(G)=\{1\}
;

4.

G
is hyperbolic and
G\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}\{1\}
;

5.

G
is an extended tower over
{\mathbb{Z}}
;

6.

there exists a free group
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}=\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\rangle
with
r\geq 2
such that
G*\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\rangle
is a simple tower over
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
;

7.

there exists a free group
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
such that
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over a non-abelian free subgroup;

8.

there exists
r\geq 2
such that the obvious embedding

{\mathbb{F}}_{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{r}*G
is elementary (see Section
8
).

Proof.

2, 3, 4 are clearly equivalent.
4\Rightarrow 5
and

6\Rightarrow 7
are clear. We prove
1\Rightarrow 2
,

5\Rightarrow 6
,

7\Rightarrow 1
, and

6\Leftrightarrow 8
.

If
G\equiv{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, it is hyperbolic by a result of Sela
[
Sel09
]
(see also
[
And18a
]
for the case with torsion)
saying that being hyperbolic is invariant under elementary equivalence among finitely generated torsion-free groups;
more simply, one can use
[
Sel01
, Corollary 4.4]

saying that limit groups containing no
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
are hyperbolic.
The implication

1\Rightarrow 2
then follows from Theorem
7.2
.

To prove
5\Rightarrow 6
, note that
G*{\mathbb{Z}}
is an extended tower over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
by Remark
4.30
. By Corollary
5.4
, there exists
s
such that
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{s}
is a simple tower over a subgroup
H<G*{\mathbb{F}}_{s}
isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. By Remark
4.30
,
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{s}*\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
is a
simple tower over
H*\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
, hence over
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
.

We now prove
7\Rightarrow 1
.
If
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is a simple tower over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, then
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
(hence also
G
) is hyperbolic by standard combination theorems
[
BF92
]
, and
G\equiv G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}\equiv{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
by Theorem
2.1
.

The equivalence between 6 and 8 follows from Theorem
2.1
(“Tarski”
)
and Theorem
8.1
below.
∎

We will see in Subsections
10.3
and
10.4
that it is necessary to use a free group
{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
in items 6, 7, 8.

8
Elementary embeddings

The previous section was devoted to elementary equivalence of abstract hyperbolic groups. In this section we consider elementary embeddings. In particular, we discuss Perin’s theorem about elementarily embedded subgroups of hyperbolic groups
[
Per11
]
, and related facts about cores and prototypes.

8.1
Characterizing elementarily embedded subgroups

Recall the following main result by Sela
[
Sel09
]
,
which implies a solution to Tarski’s problem.

.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group. If
G
is a
simple

tower
over a non-abelian subgroup
H<G
, then the inclusion
H\hookrightarrow G
is an elementary embedding.
In particular,
G
and
H
are elementarily equivalent. ∎

Recall that, if
G
is a
simple tower over
H
or, more generally, an extended tower over
H
relative to
H
, then

H
is well-defined as a subgroup of
G
(up to conjugacy); see Definition
4.36
for relative towers.

The main result of this subsection is the following theorem, where
3\Rightarrow 1
relies on “Tarski” and
1\Rightarrow 2
is the main result of Perin’s thesis
[
Per11
]
.

Theorem 8.1

(
[
Per11
]

)
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group, and let
H<G
be a non-abelian
subgroup.
The following are equivalent:

1.

H
is elementarily embedded in
G
;

2.

G
is an extended tower over
H
relative to
H
;

3.

there exists a finitely generated free group
F
such that
G*F
is a simple tower over
H
(viewed as a subgroup of
G*F
in the obvious way).

Remark 8.2

(Warnings)
.

If
G
is an extended tower over
H
relative to
H
,
the intermediate groups
G_{i}
are not elementarily embedded in
G

in general: although
G
is an extended tower over
G_{i}
