has no non-exceptional surface-type vertex. Then all subgroups
H
of
G
isomorphic to
\mathrm{c}(G)
are conjugate.

Remark 10.17
.

The subgroups
H
in the proposition need not be elementarily embedded, but it is crucial that they be isomorphic to
\mathrm{c}(G)
.

Proof.

By definition,
G
is an extended tower over
\mathrm{c}(G)
, so by Corollary
5.4
there exists a free group
F
such that

G*F
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathrm{c}(G)
.
Consider subgroups
G*F=A_{0}\supset\cdots\supset A_{p}\simeq\mathrm{c}(G)
such that, for each
i
, either
A_{i}=A_{i+1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
or
A_{i}
is a simple étage of surface type over
A_{i+1}
. Being one-ended,
A_{p}
is conjugate to a subgroup of
G
, so we may assume without loss of generality that
A_{p}<G
.

Let
H<G<A_{0}
be a subgroup isomorphic to
A_{p}
.
We will show by induction that, for all
i
, the subgroup
H
is conjugate (in
G*F
) to a subgroup of
A_{i}
.
Co-hopfianity of
A_{p}
then implies that
H
is conjugate to
A_{p}
, in
G*F
hence in
G
by malnormality of
G
, concluding the proof.

We therefore assume that
H
is conjugate into
A_{i}
. If
A_{i}=A_{i+1}*{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
H
is conjugate into
A_{i+1}
by one-endedness.
If
A_{i}
is an étage of surface type and
H
is not conjugate into the bottom group
A_{i+1}
, Lemma
5.22
and Remark
5.23
imply that
H
has a cyclic splitting with a surface-type vertex. The associated surface
\Sigma
covers a non-exceptional surface by Remark
5.23
, so is non-exceptional. Lemma
5.26
implies
that the JSJ decomposition of
H
has a surface
S
containing
\Sigma
as an incompressible subsurface. This surface
S
must be
N_{3}
or non-exceptional, contradicting our assumptions on
\mathrm{c}(G)
.
∎

Remark 10.18
.

The proof shows that every one-ended finitely generated subgroup of
G
whose cyclic JSJ decomposition has no non-exceptional surface-type vertex is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(N_{3})
or conjugate into
\mathrm{c}(G)
.

10.5
Elementary equivalence and quasi-isometry

It is well-known that elementarily equivalent groups may be very diverse. We illustrate this by comparing elementary equivalence with quasi-isometry (see
[
GdlH90
]

for basics about quasi-isometry).

Proposition 10.19
.

If
G
is any non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group, there are infinitely many groups
G_{n}
which are elementarily equivalent to
G
but are all distinct up to quasi-isometry.

Proof.

For
n\geq 3
, let
H_{n}
be the fundamental group of the space obtained by gluing
n
copies
T_{i}
of a once-punctured torus along their boundary
C_{i}
. It is a one-ended torsion-free hyperbolic group, whose canonical cyclic JSJ splitting has
n
vertices carrying the groups
\pi_{1}(T_{i})
and one vertex carrying
\pi_{1}(C_{i})
, a cyclic group independent of
i
(see Section 3.9 of
[
GL17
]
for the case
n=3
; the general case is similar).

The Bass-Serre tree of this JSJ splitting has vertices of infinite valence and vertices of valence
n
, so depends on
n
(as a simplicial tree, with no group action on it). It follows from
[
Bow98
]
that the groups
H_{n}
have non-homeomorphic Gromov boundaries, hence are quasi-isometrically distinct.
On the other hand,
H_{n}
is a simple tower over
the fundamental group of the closed orientable surface of genus 2, so is elementarily equivalent to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

In general, the groups
G_{n}=G*H_{n}
are elementarily equivalent to
G
, but are quasi-isometrically distinct by
[
PW02
]
.
∎

In the other direction, we have proved
[
GLS19
]
that, for many torsion-free hyperbolic groups
G
, there exist finite index subgroups
G_{n}
of
G
which are all distinct up to elementary equivalence (recall that finite index subgroups of
G
are quasi-isometric to
G
). This applies in particular if
G
is cubulable or is a hyperbolic limit group, but is not a free product of cyclic groups and surface groups. In full generality, we do not know whether, given a non-abelian hyperbolic group
G
, there always exist infinitely many torsion-free groups which are quasi-isometric to
G
(hence hyperbolic) but not elementarily equivalent.

10.6
Parachutes

Figure 18:
Two parachutes

We now consider parachutes, i.e. groups
G
having a simple centered splitting
\Gamma
with base
{\mathbb{Z}}
(Figure
18
).
We allow exceptional surfaces and non-retractable splittings.
These groups were studied in
[
GLS19
]
(where they were called
socket groups with identified sockets
).
They are torsion-free hyperbolic groups, and are one-ended by Lemma
5.24
.
We shall show (Corollary
10.24
) that they are either prototypes or elementarily free groups.

If the surface
\Sigma
associated to
\Gamma
has genus
g
and
b
boundary components,

G
is presented as

\left\langle\begin{matrix}a_{1},\dots,a_{b}\\
z,t_{1},\dots,t_{b}\\
u_{1},v_{1},\dots,u_{g},v_{g}\end{matrix}\ \middle|\ \begin{matrix}a_{1}\cdots a_{b}=[u_{1},v_{1}]\cdots[u_{g},v_{g}]\\
t_{i}z^{d_{i}}t_{i}^{-1}=a_{i}\\
t_{1}=1\end{matrix}\right\rangle

or

\left\langle\begin{matrix}a_{1},\dots,a_{b}\\
z,t_{1},\dots,t_{b}\\
u_{1},\dots,u_{g}\end{matrix}\ \middle|\ \begin{matrix}a_{1}\cdots a_{b}=u_{1}^{2}\cdots u_{g}^{2}\\
t_{i}z^{d_{i}}t_{i}^{-1}=a_{i}\\
t_{1}=1\end{matrix}\right\rangle,

depending on whether
\Sigma
is orientable or not, with indices
d_{i}\neq 0
(possibly negative).

Minimality of
\Gamma
implies
\sum|d_{i}|\geq 2
. The case when
\sum|d_{i}|=2
is special, since
G
is then a closed surface group
(the surface is the union of
\Sigma
and an annulus or a Möbius band).
We have seen (Example
4.35
) that such groups are elementarily free, except that
\pi_{1}(N_{3})
is a prototype.

Lemma 10.20
.

If
\sum|d_{i}|\geq 3
, then
\Gamma
is the cyclic JSJ decomposition of
G
. In particular, if
\sum|d_{i}|\geq 3
and
\Sigma
is exceptional, then
G
is a prototype.

Proof.

The proof is based on Bowditch’s construction of the cyclic JSJ tree
T_{\mathrm{can}}
from the topology of
the Gromov boundary

\partial G
of
G

[
Bow98
]
.

Let
T
be the Bass-Serre tree of
\Gamma
. Let
E
be the open star of the vertex with stabilizer
\langle z\rangle
.
It consists of exactly
N=\sum|d_{i}|
edges, all fixed by a power of
z
.

Let
\Lambda_{z}\subset\partial G
be the pair of fixed points of
\langle z\rangle
.
By
[
Bow98
]
,
\partial G\setminus\Lambda_{z}

can be partitioned into
N
disjoint open sets corresponding to the connected components of
T\setminus E
.
It follows that
\partial G\setminus\Lambda_{z}
has at least
N
components.

Since
N\geq 3
,
[
Bow98
, Proposition 5.13]
and
[
Gui00
, Lemma 4.3]
show that
\langle z\rangle
is universally elliptic (elliptic in every cyclic splitting of
G
). In particular, the edge stabilizers of
T
are universally elliptic, so
T
is dominated by the cyclic JSJ tree
T_{\mathrm{can}}
. On the other hand,
QH vertex stabilizers are elliptic in
T_{\mathrm{can}}
by
[
GL17
, Prop. 5]
, so
T
dominates

T_{\mathrm{can}}
.
It follows that
T
is a JSJ tree.
Since it is its own tree of cylinders, it coincides with
T_{\mathrm{can}}
(see
[
GL17
]
).

If
\sum|d_{i}|\geq 3
and
\Sigma
is exceptional, then

G
has no
retractable centered splitting by Lemma
5.29
, so is a prototype
(recall that, by Definition
4.23
, a retractable centered splitting is non-exceptional).
∎

Remark 10.21
.

The argument applies in the following more general setting:
G
has a centered splitting
\Gamma
with all bottom groups cyclic,
and for each bottom group the sum of the indices of the incident edge groups is at least 3.

We proved in
[
GLS19
]
that, depending on the values of
g,b
, and the
d_{i}
’s, either
\Gamma
is retractable and
G
is elementarily free, or
\Gamma
is not retractable.
When retractable, the splitting
\Gamma
expresses
G
as an extended étage over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, but not as a simple étage (see Remark
4.17
). As in Proposition
5.1
, we get a simple étage if
\Sigma
is complicated enough.

Proposition 10.22
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is retractable, and
the surface
\Sigma
either has Euler characteristic satisfying
|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
or is
a twice-punctured torus. Then
G
is a simple étage over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

The conclusion of the proposition implies that
G
has a proper elementarily embedded subgroup (isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
).
It applies whenever the
surface
\Sigma
is
not a 4-punctured sphere
S_{0,4}
, a thrice-punctured projective plane
N_{1,3}
, a twice-punctured Klein bottle
N_{2,2}
, or
N_{3,1}
(note that
\Sigma
cannot be a once-punctured torus or an exceptional surface since
\Gamma
is retractable).
In these special cases we reach the opposite conclusion:

Proposition 10.23
.

Assume that

\Sigma
has Euler characteristic satisfying

|\chi(\Sigma)|\leq 2
, and
\Sigma
is not a twice-punctured torus.
Then

G
is not a simple étage (over a non-abelian group) and

G
has no proper elementarily embedded subgroup.

Combining Lemma
5.29
, Corollary
7.10
, and the propositions, we get:

Corollary 10.24
.

Let
G
be a parachute.

•

If
\Gamma
is not retractable
(in particular if
\Sigma
is exceptional), then
G
is a prototype.

•

If
\Gamma
is retractable,
G
is elementarily free; it
has a proper elementarily embedded subgroup if and only if
\Sigma
satisfies

|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3

or is a twice-punctured torus. ∎

We shall now prove
the propositions, starting with the easier one.
They are true if
\sum|d_{i}|=2
(i.e.
G
is a surface group), as explained in Subsection
10.3
.
We therefore assume
\sum|d_{i}|\geq 3
. Lemma
10.20
then implies that
\Gamma
is the cyclic JSJ decomposition of
G
.

Proof of Proposition
10.23
.

If
\Sigma
is exceptional, then
G
is a prototype by Lemma
10.20
and the proposition holds.
We are left with the surfaces
N_{3,1}
,
N_{2,2}
,
N_{1,3}
,
S_{0,4}
.
By Theorem
8.1
,
it suffices to show that
G
is not an extended étage
over a non-abelian group relative to a non-abelian group. Suppose it is.
The associated surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
may be viewed as a proper incompressible subsurface of
\Sigma
(see Lemma
5.26
); moreover, the complement of
\Sigma^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
cannot consist only of annuli and Möbius bands because the étage is relative to a non-abelian group, so
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})=-1
.
Since
\Sigma^{\prime}
is non-exceptional and
\Sigma
is not a twice-punctured torus,
the only possibility is that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a once-punctured torus,
\Sigma
is
N_{3,1}
,
and
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
is a twice-punctured projective plane.

We then write

G=\langle u,v,u^{\prime},z\mid[u,v]u^{\prime 2}=z^{d}\rangle=\langle u,v,u^{\prime},z\mid[u,v]=z^{d}u^{\prime-2}\rangle

where
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})=\langle u,v\rangle
and the bottom group of the splitting defining the extended étage
is
\langle z,u^{\prime}\rangle\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.
But this splitting cannot be retractable since
z^{d}u^{\prime-2}
is not a commutator in
\langle z,u^{\prime}\rangle
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 10.25
.

Some of the groups in Proposition
10.23
are (non-relative) extended étages over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, but most of them are not. For instance, as in Example
10.11
, consider parachutes with
\Sigma=N_{2,2}
. If the indices
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are even,
G
is elementarily free. We claim that it is not an extended tower over
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
if
|d_{1}|\neq|d_{2}|
.

The argument is the same as in the previous proof, but we now have to consider the case when
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})=-2
. It may be a three-punctured projective plane or a 4-punctured sphere, obtained from
\Sigma
by removing one Möbius band, two Möbius bands, or a non-separating annulus. Using Lemma
4.9
, one checks that the corresponding splittings cannot be retractable.

Proof of Proposition
10.22
.

Given a non-degenerate boundary-preserving map
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
, let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a maximal family of pinched curves on
\Sigma
.
It is non-empty by Lemma
4.9
.

Case 0: there exist two boundary components
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
of
\Sigma

that are separated by
{\mathcal{C}}
.
In this case, we may argue as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
,
using an arc
\gamma
from
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
meeting a curve
\delta\in{\mathcal{C}}
exactly once.
The surface
\hat{\Sigma}
obtained by removing a pair of pants obtained as
a regular neighborhood of
C_{1}\cup\gamma\cup C_{2}
is non-exceptional because

\chi(\hat{\Sigma})=\chi(\Sigma)+1\leq-2
(when
\Sigma
is a twice-punctured torus,
\hat{\Sigma}

is a once-punctured torus, hence non-exceptional).
As in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, this defines a retractable simple centered splitting with base
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and surface
\hat{\Sigma}
,
so the proposition holds in this case.

Figure 19:
Case 1: the new étage when
\Sigma
is orientable

The remaining cases:
{\mathcal{C}}
does not separate boundary components.

We shall distinguish several cases.
In each case we define a new étage structure
by
writing the surface
\Sigma
as the union of two subsurfaces
\Sigma=\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\hat{\Sigma}
intersecting in a simple closed curve.
The surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
satisfies
\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})=-1
and contains at least one component of
\partial\Sigma
, and
\hat{\Sigma}
is associated to a simple centered splitting with base
H\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

The retractability of
\Gamma
implies
d_{1}+\dots+d_{b}=0
(and in particular
b\geq 2
) when
\Sigma
is orientable, and
d_{1}+\dots+d_{b}
even when
\Sigma
is non-orientable (
[
GLS19
]
, Propositions 6.7 and 6.8).
Using this, we
construct a retraction
\rho:G\to H
such that the central group
\pi_{1}(\hat{\Sigma})
has non-abelian image.

We use the presentation of
G
given at the beginning of this subsection, with

g
the genus of
\Sigma
and
b
the number of boundary components.
It suffices to define
\rho
on the generators
z,t_{i},u_{j},v_{j}
since
a_{i}=t_{i}z^{d_{i}}t_{i}^{-1}
.

Case 1:
\Sigma
is orientable
(see Figure
19
).
If
g=0
, then any curve in
{\mathcal{C}}
separates two boundary components of
\Sigma
and we are done.
Assume therefore that
g\geq 1
.
Recall that
b\geq 2
. Decomposing
\Sigma
into a pair of pants
\Sigma^{\prime}
with fundamental group
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
and its complement
\hat{\Sigma}
,
one gets a simple centered splitting with bottom group
H=\langle z,t_{2}\rangle

and central vertex group
Q=\langle a_{3},\dots,a_{b},u_{1},v_{1},\dots u_{g},v_{g}\rangle
. As in case 0, the surface
\hat{\Sigma}
is non-exceptional.

One then defines a retraction
\rho:G\to H
by

\displaystyle z,t_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto z,t_{2}

\displaystyle t_{3},\dots,t_{b},u_{2},\dots,u_{g},v_{2},\dots,v_{g}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1

and there remains to define the images
\tilde{u}_{1}
,
\tilde{v}_{1}\in H
of
u_{1},v_{1}
satisfying the equation

z^{d_{1}}t_{2}z^{d_{2}}t_{2}^{-1}z^{d_{3}+\dots+d_{b}}=[\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{v}_{1}].

Since
d_{1}+\dots+d_{b}=0
, we may take
\tilde{u}_{1}=z^{d_{1}}t_{2}
and
\tilde{v}_{1}=z^{d_{2}}
.
Finally,
\rho(Q)
contains
\langle\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{v}_{1}\rangle
,
which is non-abelian since
d_{2}\neq 0
.

Figure 20:
Cases 2 and 3:
\Sigma
is non-orientable of genus
g\geq 3

Case 2:
\Sigma
is non-orientable with a single boundary component
(see Figure
20
).
Note that
|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
implies
g\geq 4
,
and recall that
d_{1}
is even.
In this case, we take for
\Sigma^{\prime}
a twice-punctured projective plane containing
\partial\Sigma

(see Figure
20
).
Its complement
\hat{\Sigma}
in
\Sigma
is homeomorphic to
N_{g-1,1}
, hence non-exceptional because
g\geq 4
.
The group
G
has a simple centered splitting with bottom group
H=\langle z,u_{1}\rangle
and
central vertex group
Q=\pi_{1}(\hat{\Sigma})=\langle u_{2},\dots,u_{g}\rangle
.

One defines
\rho:G\to H
by

\displaystyle z,u_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto z,u_{1}

\displaystyle u_{4},\dots,u_{g}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1,

and there remains to define the images
\tilde{u}_{2}
,
\tilde{u}_{3}
of
u_{2},u_{3}
satisfying the equation

z^{d_{1}}=u_{1}^{2}\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}\tilde{u}_{3}^{2}
.
Since
d_{1}
is even and non-zero, one may take
\tilde{u}_{2}=u_{1}^{-1}
and
\tilde{u}_{3}=z^{d_{1}/2}
,
and
\rho(Q)=\langle\tilde{u}_{2},\tilde{u}_{3}\rangle
is non-abelian.

Case 3:
\Sigma
is non-orientable of genus
g\geq 3
with
b\geq 2
boundary components

(see Figure
20
).
As in case 1, we take for
\Sigma^{\prime}
a pair of pants,
and we get a simple centered splitting of
G
with bottom group
H=\langle z,t_{2}\rangle

and central vertex group
Q=\langle a_{3},\dots,a_{b},u_{1},\dots,u_{g}\rangle
.

One defines
\rho

by

\displaystyle z,t_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto z,t_{2}

\displaystyle t_{3},\dots,t_{b},u_{4},\dots,u_{g}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1

and there remains to define the images
\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{u}_{2},\tilde{u}_{3}
of
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
in
H
satisfying the equation

\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}\tilde{u}_{3}^{2}=z^{d_{1}}t_{2}z^{d_{2}}t_{2}^{-1}z^{d_{3}+\dots+d_{b}}=[z^{d_{1}}t_{2},z^{d_{2}}]z^{2k}

with
d_{1}+\dots+d_{b}=2k
.
This is possible since
[a,b]c^{2}
is a product of three squares in any group
(e.g.
[a,b]c^{2}=(ac^{-1}b)^{2}(b^{-1}c)^{2}(c^{-1}ba^{-1}b^{-1}c^{2})^{2}
,
which can be viewed as a consequence of the homeomorphism
T^{2}\#P^{2}\simeq P^{2}\#P^{2}\#P^{2}
).
One may thus take
\tilde{u}_{1}=z^{d_{1}}t_{2}z^{d_{2}-k}
,
\tilde{u}_{2}=z^{k-d_{2}}
, and
\tilde{u}_{3}=z^{-k+d_{2}}t_{2}^{-1}z^{2k-d_{1}-d_{2}}
.
Note that

\langle\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{u}_{2},\tilde{u}_{3}\rangle
is non-abelian unless
k=d_{2}
and
2k-d_{2}=0
, i.e.
k=d_{2}=0
, which is impossible.

Figure 21:
Case 4:
\Sigma
is non-orientable with genus
g\leq 2
, and
b\geq 3

Case 4:
\Sigma
is non-orientable of genus
g=2
(see Figure
21
).
Since
|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
, we have
b\geq 3
.
Since
d_{1}+\dots+d_{b}
is even,
one may assume (after renumbering) that
d_{2}
or
d_{2}+d_{3}
is even.
As above, one may take for
\Sigma^{\prime}
a pair of pants, so that
G
has a simple centered splitting with bottom group
H=\langle z,t_{2}\rangle
.
One defines
\rho

by

\displaystyle z,t_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto z,t_{2}

\displaystyle t_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1\text{ or }t_{2}

\displaystyle t_{4},\dots,t_{b}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1,

where we map
t_{3}
to
1
if
d_{2}
is even and to
t_{2}
otherwise. We treat the case where
d_{2}
is even,
the other case being similar.
To complete the definition of
\rho
, we need to find images
\tilde{u}_{1},\tilde{u}_{2}
of
u_{1},u_{2}
in
H

which satisfy the equation

z^{d_{1}}t_{2}z^{d_{2}}t_{2}^{-1}z^{d_{3}+\dots+d_{b}}=\tilde{u}_{1}^{2}\tilde{u}_{2}^{2}.

This is possible because
d_{2}
and
d_{3}+\dots+d_{b}+d_{1}
are even.
Since
\rho(a_{1}a_{2})=z^{d_{1}}t_{2}z^{d_{2}}t_{2}^{-1}
does not commute with
\rho(a_{3})
, which is conjugate to
z^{d_{3}}
, the image
\rho(Q)
is non-abelian.

Case 5:
\Sigma
is non-orientable of genus 1.
We show that this cannot happen under the assumption that
{\mathcal{C}}
does not separate boundary components.
Being two-sided, all curves in
{\mathcal{C}}
are separating. The components of
\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{C}}
which do not meet
\partial\Sigma

are punctured projective planes or spheres bounded by pinched curves, and their fundamental group
has trivial image under
p:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to G
because
G
is torsion-free. The connected component containing
\partial\Sigma
has cyclic image by Lemma
4.9
and
this forces
p
to have an abelian image, a contradiction.
∎

Multi-parachutes.

More generally, suppose that
G
has a (possibly
non-simple) centered splitting
\Gamma
with bottom groups all isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}
. We call such a group a multi-parachute. For instance, one may connect all boundary components of
\Sigma
to distinct cyclic vertex groups; groups obtained in this way were studied in
[
GLS19
]
under the name of socket groups, and elementarily free socket groups are described in
[
GLS19
, Proposition 6.2]
.

We now study general multi-parachutes. In particular, we show that they are either prototypes or elementarily free groups, and we determine whether
they have proper elementarily embedded subgroups.

Like parachutes, multi-parachutes are one-ended hyperbolic groups.
After gluing annuli and Möbius bands to the surface if needed, we may assume that
\sum d_{i}(v)\geq 3
for each bottom vertex
v
(we denote by
d_{i}(v)
the indices of incident edge groups in the vertex group), so
\Gamma
is the cyclic JSJ decomposition of
G
by Lemma
10.20
and Remark
10.21
.
The case of surface groups and parachutes has already been studied, so assume that
\Gamma
has at least two (cyclic) bottom groups.

If
\Gamma
is not retractable (in particular if
\Sigma
is exceptional),
G
is a prototype by Lemma
5.29
,
so we now assume that
\Gamma
is retractable. In this case
G
is an extended étage over a free group, hence elementarily free by Corollary
7.10
. We distinguish three cases, depending on
\Sigma
.

\bullet
If

|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
, Proposition
5.1
shows that
G
is a simple tower over a non-abelian free group,
thus providing an elementarily embedded proper free subgroup by “Tarski”.

\bullet

If

\Sigma
is a twice-punctured torus, then
\Gamma
has to be simple
by Proposition 6.2 of
[
GLS19
]
(see Remark
4.25
).
Thus
G
is a parachute, and it has an elementarily embedded proper free subgroup by Proposition
10.22
.

\bullet
If

|\chi(\Sigma)|\leq 2
with
\Sigma
non-exceptional, not a twice-punctured torus,
then
G
is not an extended étage
over a non-abelian group relative to a non-abelian group (so has no proper elementarily embedded subgroup by Theorem
8.1
).
This is proved in the same way as Proposition
10.23
.

We have proved the following generalisation of Corollary
10.24
.

Corollary 10.26
.

Let
\Gamma
be a multi-parachute with
\sum d_{i}(v)\geq 3
at each bottom vertex.

•

If
\Gamma
is not retractable
(in particular if
\Sigma
is exceptional), then
G
is a prototype.

•

If
\Gamma
is retractable, then
G
is elementarily free; in this case, it
has a
proper elementarily embedded subgroup if and only if
|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
or
\Sigma
is a twice-punctured torus. ∎

One may decide under what conditions
\Gamma
is retractable. This is done in Propositions 6.2, 6.7, and 6.8 of
[
GLS19
]
for parachutes and socket groups. The other cases may also be worked out, we leave details to the reader.
The complete list of elementarily free multi-parachutes having no proper elementarily embedded subgroup (called minimal in the next section) is shown on Figure
22
.
We will see that, together with
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
, this
describes all minimal finitely generated elementarily free groups (Theorem
11.4
).

Figure 22:
The complete list of finitely generated, minimal, elementarily free groups other than
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
and
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
;
the sum of the indices at each cyclic group should be
\geq 3
.

11
Minimal and maximal models

Although the definitions and facts that follow make sense in the broader context of any first-order structure in a countable language, we restrict ourselves to groups (in the first-order language of groups). We refer to
[
Mar02
,
Hod93
]
for basic facts.

A group is called
minimal
if it has no proper elementarily embedded subgroup. For example, the group of integers is minimal.

A group
H

is called
prime
if it elementarily embeds into any other group elementarily equivalent to it; equivalently,

H
is countable and, for any tuple
\underline{h}\in H^{k}
, there is a formula
\sigma
(without constants) such that, given another tuple
\underline{h}^{\prime}\in H^{k}
,
one has
H\models\sigma(\underline{h}^{\prime})
if and only if

\underline{h}
and
\underline{h}^{\prime}
are in the same
{\mathrm{Aut}}(H)
-orbit;
in other words,
H
is countable and, for all
k\geq 1
and all tuples
\underline{h}\in H^{k}
, the
{\mathrm{Aut}}(H)
-orbit of
\underline{h}
is
\emptyset
-definable (i.e. definable without constants).

We shall characterize
prime torsion-free hyperbolic groups and minimal elementarily free groups.

A countable group
H
is
homogeneous
if, for any
k\geq 1
,
any two tuples
\underline{h},\underline{h}^{\prime}\in H^{k}
satisfying the same first-order formulas (without constants) are in the same
{\mathrm{Aut}}(H)
-orbit.
Equivalently, the
{\mathrm{Aut}}(H)
-orbit of every finite tuple
is
\emptyset
-type definable
, i.e. definable by infinitely many first-order formulas (without constants).
Prime groups are homogeneous; conversely, finitely generated free groups are homogeneous
[
PS12
]
but not prime.

A sequence of elementary embeddings
H_{1}\preceq_{e}H_{2}\preceq_{e}\dots\preceq_{e}H_{n}\preceq_{e}\cdots
is called
an
elementary chain
of groups.

Using a Fraïssé construction,
we prove that,
given a non-abelian
torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
, there exists a homogeneous countable (infinitely generated) group
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}\equiv G

which is the union of an elementary chain of finitely generated subgroups and in which all finitely generated groups elementarily equivalent to
G
embed elementarily; moreover,
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}
is unique up to isomorphism.
This extends Theorem 5.9 of
[
KMS18
]
.

11.1
Primality

Primality is characterized as follows.

Theorem 11.1
.

A non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
is prime if and only if
G
is a one-ended prototype.

Proof.

We first prove that prime groups are prototypes.
If
G
is prime and elementarily free, it embeds elementarily into
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
, hence is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
by Corollary
8.3
.
But
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
is not prime because it does not embed elementarily into
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
by Lemma
10.12
(though
\pi_{1}(N_{4})\equiv{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
). This proves that no elementarily free group is prime.

Now suppose that
G
is prime and not elementarily free (so that
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)=\mathrm{c}(G)\neq\{1\}
). Since
G
is elementarily equivalent to
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
by Corollary
7.7
,
it embeds elementarily into
\mathrm{\widetilde{c}}(G)
by primeness.
But prototypes have no proper elementarily embedded subgroup by Corollary
8.9
, so this embedding is an isomorphism and
G
must be a prototype.
This proves that only prototypes may be prime.

Theorem
10.13
implies that infinitely-ended prototypes are not prime, and we
complete the proof by showing that any one-ended prototype
P
is prime.
By Corollary
8.8
,

P
elementarily embeds into any
finitely generated
(hence hyperbolic by
[
Sel09
]
) group
G
elementarily equivalent to
P
.
This is not enough because it does not apply if
G
is an infinitely generated
group, so we quote
[
PS16
, Th. 5.3]
to deduce that
P
is prime.
∎

Though infinitely-ended prototypes are not prime,
they satisfy a weak “prime model” property.

Proposition 11.2

(Weak prime models)
.

A non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
is the free product of a prototype and a free group if and only if, for any finitely generated group
K
elementarily equivalent to
G
,
the group
G
elementarily embeds into
K*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
for some
r\geq 0
.

Example 11.3
.

This applies to the free group
G={\mathbb{F}}_{n}
for
n\geq 2
(see Corollary
7.10
).

Proof.

Suppose
G=P*F^{1}
with
P
a prototype and
F^{1}
free. If
K\equiv G
, then
P
is the core of
K
, so
K\mathrel{\geqslant_{t}}P
. By Corollary
5.4
,
there exist free groups
F^{2},F^{3}
such that
K*F^{2}
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
P*F^{3}
,
so
K*F^{2}*F^{1}
is a simple tower over a subgroup isomorphic to
P*F^{3}*F^{1}
and the result follows.

To prove the converse, we let
K
be the core
c(G)
and we assume that
G
elementarily embeds into
c(G)*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
. By Proposition
8.10
,
G
is a co-free free factor of
c(G)*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
, hence is isomorphic to the free product of
c(G)
with a free group.
∎

11.2
Minimality

We saw that prototypes are minimal
(Corollary
8.9
). Conversely:

Theorem 11.4
.

Let
G
be a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group.

1.

If
\mathrm{c}(G)
is one-ended, then
G
is minimal if and only if
G=\mathrm{c}(G)
(i.e.
G
is a prototype).

2.

If
\mathrm{c}(G)
is infinitely-ended, there are infinitely many minimal hyperbolic groups elementarily equivalent to
G
.

3.

If
G
is elementarily free, then
G
is minimal if and only if
G
is isomorphic
to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
,
\pi_{1}(N_{4})
, or one of the multi-parachutes shown on Figure
22
.

In particular, there are infinitely many minimal groups elementarily equivalent to
G
(up to isomorphism) if and only if
\mathrm{c}(G)
is
trivial or infinitely ended.

Proof.

Assertion 1
follows from Corollaries
8.8
and
8.9
.
If
\mathrm{c}(G)
is infinitely-ended, we write its Grushko decomposition
\mathrm{c}(G)\simeq A_{1}*\cdots*A_{p}
with
p\geq 2
and we
consider
G(a_{1},a_{2})=K(A_{1},A_{2},a_{1},a_{2})*A_{3}*\cdots*A_{p}
as in
Theorem
10.13
. We have seen that
G(a_{1},a_{2})
is minimal and elementarily equivalent to
G
if
a_{i}
is a square and
A_{i}
has no cyclic splitting relative to
a_{i}
.

In order to get infinitely many different groups, we fix
a_{1},a_{2}
and for
n\geq 1
we consider
G_{n}=G(a_{1}^{n},a_{2}^{n})=K(A_{1},A_{2},a_{1}^{n},a_{2}^{n})*A_{3}*\cdots*A_{p}
. These groups are non-isomorphic because by Remark
10.6
their canonical JSJ decompositions are different: they are distinguished by indices of edge groups in vertex groups.

To prove Assertion 3, suppose that
G
is elementarily free, minimal, not
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. It cannot have a cyclic free factor, so it is an extended étage of surface type.
Let
\Gamma
be the associated splitting. First suppose that the associated surface satisfies
|\chi(\Sigma)|\geq 3
. By Propositions
5.1

(if
\Gamma
is not simple) and
10.22
(if
G
is a parachute),
G
is a simple étage over a non-abelian group so is not minimal. Thus
|\chi(\Sigma)|\leq 2
.

Since
G
is elementarily free, all bottom groups have trivial core.
If some bottom group
H
is non-abelian, then

G
is a tower over
H
relative to
H
so is not minimal by Theorem
8.1
.
It follows that
G
is an étage with all bottom groups isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e. a multi-parachute.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the surface obtained by gluing to
\Sigma
all annuli or Möbius bands corresponding to bottom vertices
v
with
\sum d_{i}(v)=2
. It satisfies
|\chi(\Sigma^{\prime})|\leq 2
.

If it is closed,
it must be
N_{4}
because

\pi_{1}(N_{3})
is not elementarily free and
\pi_{1}(S_{2})
contains an elementarily embedded subgroup isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
. Thus
G\simeq\pi_{1}(N_{4})
.
Otherwise, we get a new multi-parachute
with
\sum d_{i}(v)\geq 3
at each vertex.
As explained at the end of Section
10
,
G
is on the list of Figure
22
.

This proves one direction of Assertion 3. The converse follows from Corollary
8.3
(if
G
is free), Lemma
10.12
(if
G\simeq\pi_{1}(N_{4})
), and Corollary
10.26
(if
G
is a multi-parachute).
∎

11.3
Universality

This subsection is devoted to the following result.

Theorem 11.5
.

Given any non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
,
there exists a unique countable (infinitely generated) group
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}
elementarily equivalent to
G
with the following properties:

1.

{\mathcal{M}}_{G}
is homogeneous;

2.

there exists an elementary chain of finitely generated subgroups
G_{1}\preceq_{e}G_{2}\preceq_{e}\dots\preceq_{e}G_{n}\preceq_{e}\cdots

such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}G_{i}
;

3.

all torsion-free hyperbolic groups elementarily equivalent to
G
elementarily embed into
{\mathcal{M}}_{G}
.

The proof relies on a Fraïssé construction.
The following definition and Theorem
11.9
extend Fraïssé’s original result. The theorem can be proved following the same line of thought and is considered folklore; nevertheless, a more detailed explanation is given in Section 2.3 of
[
KMS18
]
.

Definition 11.6

(strong elementary Fraïssé)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a
non-empty class of finitely generated groups
which is countable (up to isomorphism) and
has the following properties:

•

the class
\mathcal{K}
is closed under isomorphism
(isomorphism property, IP)
;

•

the class
\mathcal{K}
is closed under passing to finitely generated elementarily embedded subgroups,
i.e. if
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
K^{\prime}\preceq_{e}K

is a finitely generated elementarily embedded subgroup
of
K
, then
K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}

(elementary hereditary property, e-HP)
;

•

if
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
are in
\mathcal{K}
, then there is
K
in
\mathcal{K}

and elementary embeddings
f_{i}:K_{i}\rightarrow K
for
i\leq 2

(elementary joint embedding property, e-JEP)
;

•

if
K_{0}
,
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
are in
\mathcal{K}
and
f_{i}:A\rightarrow K_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, are partial elementary maps of
some finitely generated subgroup
A\subset K_{0}
,
then there is a group
K
in
\mathcal{K}
and elementary embeddings
g_{i}:K_{i}\rightarrow K
for
i=1,2
with
g_{1}\circ f_{1}=g_{2}\circ f_{2}

(strong
elementary amalgamation property, strong e-AP)
.

Then
\mathcal{K}
is a
strong elementary Fraïssé class
(strong
e
-Fraïssé class for short).

See Definition
9.1
for partial elementary maps.

Remark 11.7
.

Note that, by e-JEP, all groups in
{\mathcal{K}}
are elementarily equivalent.
When

A=\{1\}
, strong e-AP reduces to e-JEP.

Remark 11.8
.

Strong e-AP can be reformulated as follows with no reference to
K_{0}
:
given
K_{1},K_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}
, a finitely generated subgroup
A_{1}\subset K_{1}
and a partial elementary map
f:A_{1}\rightarrow K_{2}
,
there exists a group
K\in{\mathcal{K}}
and elementary embeddings
g_{i}:K_{i}\rightarrow K
for
i=1,2
with
g_{1}=g_{2}\circ f
.

Theorem 11.9
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a strong
e
-Fraïssé class. There exists a countable group

\mathcal{M}
such that:

•

the class of finitely generated groups that elementarily embed in
{\mathcal{M}}
is exactly
\mathcal{K}
;

•

the group
\mathcal{M}
is homogeneous;

•

the group
\mathcal{M}
is the union of an elementary chain of groups
in
\mathcal{K}
.

Moreover, any other countable group with the above properties is isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}
. ∎

Using this theorem, we prove Theorem
11.5
by
showing that, given a torsion-free hyperbolic group
G
, the class
\mathcal{K}_{G}
of all finitely generated groups
elementarily equivalent to
G

is an elementary Fraïssé class. We just have to check the four properties of Definition
11.6
.
Recall that
{\mathcal{K}}_{G}
consists of torsion-free hyperbolic groups
[
Sel09
]
.

The isomorphism property (IP) and the elementary hereditary property (e-HP) are obvious. The elementary joint embedding property (e-JEP) follows from the first assertion of Proposition
8.11
,
which was proved as follows: if
G_{1}\equiv G_{2}
, then their cores
H_{1},H_{2}
are isomorphic by Theorem
7.2
, and
elementarily embedded in
G^{\prime}_{1}=G_{1}*{\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1}}
,
G^{\prime}_{2}=G_{2}*{\mathbb{F}}_{r_{2}}
for some free groups
{\mathbb{F}}_{r_{1}},{\mathbb{F}}_{r_{2}}
.
We then defined
G^{\prime}=G^{\prime}_{1}*_{H_{1}=H_{2}}G^{\prime}_{2}
.

The strong
elementary amalgamation property (strong e-AP)
may be viewed as a relative version of (e-JEP). It is proved in a similar way, using Remark
11.8
and replacing Theorem
7.2
by Theorem
9.7
.

Appendix A
Appendix: Towers
are limit groups

The goal of this section is to prove Corollary
A.2
saying that towers over a torsion-free hyperbolic
group
H
are
H
-limit groups. This basic fact is stated in
[
Sel09
]
, but some details of the proof are missing.
The same arguments show that, if a finitely generated group has a strict resolution over a hyperbolic group
H
, then it is an
H
-limit group.

Theorem A.1
.

If
G
is a simple étage of surface type over a torsion-free hyperbolic group
H
, there exists a discriminating sequence of retractions
\rho_{n}:G\rightarrow H
.

Discriminating means that, given any finite subset
F\subset G
, the map
\rho_{n}
is injective on
F
for all
n
large enough. Recall that
H
must be non-abelian (Remark
4.17
).

Corollary A.2
.

If
G
is a
tower (simple or extended) over a non-abelian torsion-free hyperbolic group
H
, then it is an
H
-limit group.

We first deduce the corollary from the theorem. The remainder of this appendix will be devoted to the proof of the theorem.

Proof.

First suppose that
G>G_{1}>\dots>G_{n}=H
is a simple tower.
All groups
G_{i}
are hyperbolic, and there exists a discriminating sequence of retractions from
G_{i+1}
to
G_{i}
by Theorem
A.1

(it is well known that this also holds if
G_{i+1}=G_{i}*{\mathbb{Z}}
is an étage of free product type with
G_{i}
non-abelian,
by sending the generator of the cyclic factor to a sequence of small cancellation elements,
see for instance
[
And19
, Th. 1.9]
).
Composing these retractions gives a discriminating sequence of retractions from
G
to
H
, so
G
is an
H
-limit group.

If the tower is not simple, some
G*{\mathbb{F}}_{r}
is an
H
-limit group by Corollary
5.4
,
and
G
is an
H
-limit group because it is a finitely generated subgroup of an
H
-limit group.
∎

A.1
Applying Baumslag’s lemma

To
prove Theorem
A.1
, we first need the following generalization of Baumslag’s lemma
[
Bau67
]
. For
g\neq 1
in
H
, we denote by
g^{\pm\infty}=\lim_{n\to\pm\infty}g^{n}
the limit of
g^{n}
in the Gromov boundary
\partial H
.

Lemma A.3

(
[
And18a
, Prop. 2.19]
)
.

Let
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k},c_{1},\dots,c_{k}

be elements of a torsion-free hyperbolic group
H
, with
c_{i}\neq 1
.
Assume that
a_{i}\,c_{i+1}^{+\infty}\neq c_{i}^{-\infty}
for
i=1,\dots,k-1
.
Then there exists
C\geq 0
such that
the element

a_{0}c_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{1}\cdots c_{k}^{p_{k}}a_{k}

is non-trivial for all
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\geq C
.∎

Corollary A.4
.

Suppose that a torsion-free hyperbolic group
G

is the fundamental group of a graph of
groups
\Gamma
such that all edge groups are infinite cyclic, and maximal cyclic in adjacent vertex groups (hence in
G
). Let
\hat{\tau}\in{\mathrm{Out}}(G)
be the product of the twists around all edges of
\Gamma
, and
\tau
a representative of
\hat{\tau}
in
{\mathrm{Aut}}(G)
.

If
f:G\rightarrow H
is a homomorphism which is injective on each vertex group of
\Gamma
, with

H
a torsion-free hyperbolic group,
then the sequence of homomorphisms
f\circ\tau^{n}:G\rightarrow H
is discriminating.

Edge groups
G_{e}
being infinite cyclic, each edge
e
carries a twist
\hat{\tau}_{e}
; the pair
\{\hat{\tau}_{e},\hat{\tau}_{e}^{-1}\}
is well-defined in
{\mathrm{Out}}(G)
, and it does not matter for the theorem whether we use
\hat{\tau}_{e}
or
\hat{\tau}_{e}^{-1}
to define
\hat{\tau}
, or which representative
\tau
of
\hat{\tau}
in
{\mathrm{Aut}}(G)
we use.

The proof of Corollary
A.4
is a standard application of Baumslag’s lemma (see for instance
[
CG05
, Prop. 4.11]
).
We prove it in the case of an amalgam
G=A*_{\langle c\rangle}B
and leave the general case to the reader.

Proof in the case of an amalgam.

We choose the representative
\tau
of
\hat{\tau}
equal to the identity on
A
and to conjugation by
c
on
B
. It suffices to prove that, given any
g\in G\setminus\{1\}
, we have
(f\circ\tau^{n})(g)\neq 1
for all
n
large enough.
This is obvious if
g
is contained in
A
or
B
(up to conjugacy).
Otherwise, up to conjugation,
g
may be written as
g=a_{1}b_{1}\dots a_{k}b_{k}
with
a_{i}\in A_{i}\setminus\langle c\rangle
and
b_{i}\in B_{i}\setminus\langle c\rangle
, and
k\geq 1
.

Now

\tau^{n}(g)=a_{1}c^{n}b_{1}c^{-n}a_{2}c^{n}\dots c^{-n}a_{k}c^{n}b_{k}c^{-n}

and

(f\circ\tau^{n})(g)=f(a_{1})f(c)^{n}f(b_{1})f(c)^{-n}\cdots f(a_{k})f(c)^{n}f(b_{k})f(c)^{-n}.

By assumption,
\langle c\rangle
is maximal abelian in
A
and in
B
, so

a_{i}
and
b_{i}
do not commute with
c
.
Since
f
is injective on
A
and
B
, the elements
f(a_{i})
and
f(b_{i})
do not commute with
f(c)
,
so none of the elements
f(a_{i}).f(c)^{\pm\infty},f(b_{i}).f(c)^{\pm\infty}
belongs to
\{f(c)^{\pm\infty}\}
.
Thus Lemma
A.3
applies and concludes the proof.
∎

The idea to prove Theorem
A.1
is to apply Corollary
A.4
to the centered splitting
\Gamma
associated to the étage, with
f
a retraction
\rho:G\to H
, but there are two problems. First, the edge groups of
\Gamma
, though maximal cyclic subgroups of the central group, do not have to be maximal cyclic in bottom groups (as in Example
10.2
). This is taken care of by introducing surfaces with sockets. The second problem is that the retraction
\rho:G\to H
to the bottom group of
\Gamma
does not have to be injective on the central group. To fix this we will have to refine
\Gamma
, using a multicurve on the surface associated to
\Gamma
, so that
\rho
is injective on the new vertex groups.

A.2
Surfaces with sockets

A
surface group with sockets

\hat{Q}
is the amalgam of the fundamental group of a compact hyperbolic surface
\Sigma

with cyclic groups over boundary subgroups of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. More precisely, it is the fundamental group
of a (possibly non-minimal) tree of groups
\Lambda
with a central surface-type vertex
v
(in the sense of Definition
3.5
) carrying the group
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
for some hyperbolic surface
\Sigma
,
and terminal vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
carrying
{\mathbb{Z}}
. We say that
\hat{Q}
is
non-exceptional
if the surface
\Sigma
is non-exceptional.

This is similar to a centered splitting with all bottom groups
{\mathbb{Z}}
, with two differences. First, there is a single edge
e_{i}
joining
v
to a given
v_{i}
.
Second,
\Lambda
does not have to be minimal:
G_{e_{i}}
may be equal to
G_{v_{i}}
; if this does not happen,
\hat{Q}
is a
particular kind of a multi-parachute in the sense of Subsection
10.6
.

The group carried by
e_{i}
is the fundamental group of a component
C_{i}
of
\partial\Sigma
, and
C_{i}\neq C_{j}
for
i\neq j
. It has finite index
d_{i}
in the vertex group
G_{v_{i}}
. We allow

d_{i}=1
(i.e.
G_{e_{i}}=G_{v_{i}}
) and
d_{i}=2

(this corresponds to gluing a Möbius band
to
C_{i}
).

In topological terms,
\hat{Q}
is the fundamental group of a graph of spaces
X_{\hat{Q}}
obtained by gluing each boundary component
C_{i}
to a circle by a covering of degree
d_{i}
.

The
socket subgroups
of
\hat{Q}
are the conjugates of the vertex groups
G_{v_{i}}
.
A homomorphism from
\hat{Q}
to an arbitrary group is injective on each boundary subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
if and only if it is injective on each socket subgroup of
\hat{Q}
.

An
essential multicurve
in
\Sigma
is a collection
{\mathcal{C}}
of disjoint non-parallel 2-sided simple closed curves, each of which is not boundary parallel
and does not bound a Möbius band or a disc.
The cyclic splitting of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
dual to
{\mathcal{C}}
induces a cyclic splitting of
\hat{Q}
. The
vertex groups of this splitting
are fundamental groups of connected components of
X_{\hat{Q}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}
, they have
a natural structure of a surface with sockets (curves in
{\mathcal{C}}
give rise to sockets of index 1).

A.3
A reduction

Following Sela, the proof of Theorem
A.1
is based on the following result.

Theorem A.5
.

Let
\hat{Q}
be a non-exceptional surface group with sockets, with underlying surface
\Sigma
, and let
H
be a torsion-free hyperbolic group.
Let
f:\hat{Q}\rightarrow H
be a homomorphism with non-abelian image, which is injective on each socket subgroup of
\hat{Q}
.

There exists an essential multicurve
{\mathcal{C}}
in
\Sigma
such that
the restriction of
f

to the fundamental group of each connected component of
X_{\hat{Q}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}

is injective.

Remark A.6
.

Theorem
A.5
also applies if
\Sigma
is the closed non-orientable surface
N_{3}
of genus
3
(with no sockets), see Remark
A.10
.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
from Theorem
A.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be the simple centered splitting of
G
associated to the étage, with bottom group
H
and central group
Q
.
Perform folds to transform
\Gamma
into
a splitting
\hat{\Gamma}
over maximal cyclic subgroups; this replaces
Q
by a surface group with sockets
\hat{Q}
without changing

H
(compare Remark
10.6
, where one obtains
\hat{\Gamma}
from
\Gamma_{\!\mathrm{can}}
by collapsing the edges
vv_{i}
).

Let
\rho:G\rightarrow H
be a retraction associated to the étage, as in Proposition
4.14
. Its restriction to
\hat{Q}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
A.5
, so we can
refine the splitting
\hat{\Gamma}
using the essential multicurve provided by the theorem.
This provides a new splitting
\hat{\Gamma}_{c}
over maximal cyclic subgroups such that
\rho
is injective in restriction
to every vertex group.

We apply Corollary
A.4
to this splitting.
Since
H
is a vertex group of
\hat{\Gamma}_{c}
, the outer automorphism
\hat{\tau}
has a representative
\tau
in
{\mathrm{Aut}}(G)
which
is the identity on
H
(the assumption that the étage is simple is used here).
Thus the maps
\rho_{n}:=\rho\circ\tau^{n}:G\rightarrow H
are retractions. By Corollary
A.4
, this sequence of retractions is discriminating.
∎

The following subsections are devoted to the proof of Theorem
A.5
.
We first prove in subsection
A.4
that one can decompose the surface
\Sigma
into pairs of pants and twice-punctured projective planes whose fundamental groups
all have non-abelian image. We then prove the theorem in Subsection
A.5
by using the fact that
\Sigma
is non-exceptional and
reducing the general case to two special cases:
the once-punctured torus and the 4-punctured sphere. The proof in these special cases requires ping-pong lemmas which are proved in Subsection
A.6
.

A.4
Decomposing into pieces with non-abelian image

The first step in the proof of Theorem
A.5
is the following lemma. Recall (Definition
3.4
) that a two-sided simple closed curve is pinched under a homomorphism if its fundamental group is contained in the kernel.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
H
be a torsion-free CSA group,
and let
f:\hat{Q}\rightarrow H
be a homomorphism with non-abelian image, with
\hat{Q}
a surface group with sockets. Assume that no component of
\partial\Sigma
is pinched.

There exists an essential multicurve
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}

consisting of non-pinched curves
such that every connected component of
\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{0}

is a pair of pants or a twice-punctured projective plane whose fundamental group has non-abelian image under
f
.

We do not assume that the underlying surface
\Sigma
is non-exceptional.

Remark A.8
.

As a preliminary remark, we note that, if
\hat{Q}
is obtained from a
group
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
by adding sockets,
and
f:\hat{Q}\rightarrow H
takes values in a torsion-free commutative
transitive group,
then
f(\hat{Q})
is non-abelian if and only if
f(Q)
is
non-abelian. We give the argument when there is only one socket. Write
\hat{Q}=\langle Q,u\rangle
with
u^{d}\in Q
, and assume that
f(Q)
is abelian.
If
f(u^{d})

is trivial, so is
f(u)
because
H
is torsion-free, and
f(\hat{Q})=f(Q)
is abelian.
Otherwise, both
f(Q)
and
f(u)
commute with
f(u^{d})
, hence
commute by commutative transitivity, so
f(\hat{Q})
is abelian.

This remark shows that we may forget about sockets in Lemma
A.7
:
it suffices to prove the lemma for the fundamental group of a surface with boundary.
It is an immediate consequence of the following.

Lemma A.9

(
[
Sel01
, Lemma 5.13]
,
[
Sel09
, Lemma 1.32]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a
compact surface
which is not homeomorphic to a pair of pants or a twice-punctured projective plane. Let
H
be a torsion-free CSA group,
and
f:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow H
a homomorphism with non-abelian image. Assume that no component of
\partial\Sigma
is pinched.

There exists a non-pinched essential two-sided simple closed curve
\gamma\subset\Sigma

such that the fundamental group of every connected component of
\Sigma\setminus\gamma
has
non-abelian image under
f
.

Proof.

Let
Q=\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. We start with two observations.

Obervation 1:
if there exists a non-separating two-sided non-pinched curve
\gamma
,
then the fundamental group
J
of

\Sigma\setminus\gamma
automatically has
non-abelian image.
Indeed, one has an HNN decomposition
Q=J*_{\pi_{1}(\gamma)}
and, if
f(J)
is abelian,
then the image of the stable letter has to commute with
f(J)
by the CSA property.

The lemma follows in the case when
\Sigma
is orientable with at most one boundary component, because
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)

is generated by fundamental groups of non-separating essential simple closed curves.

Observation 2:
if
\Sigma
contains a two-sided non-separating simple closed curve
\gamma
which is pinched, and
\partial\Sigma\neq\emptyset
,
one can find another such curve
\gamma^{\prime}
which is non-pinched
as follows:
take an arc
I
joining
\gamma
to a boundary component
b
, and consider the curve
\gamma^{\prime}
obtained as the connected sum of
b
and
\gamma
along
I
.
Since
\gamma
is pinched, the elements of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
corresponding to
b
and
\gamma^{\prime}
have conjugate images under
f
, so
\gamma^{\prime}
is not pinched.
Moreover, if
I
is chosen disjoint from some simple closed curve
\delta
with
i(\delta,\gamma)=1
,
then
i(\delta,\gamma^{\prime})=1
so
\gamma^{\prime}
is non-separating.

Using these two observations repeatedly, we see that there remain three cases to analyze:
the
n
-punctured sphere (
n\geq 4
), the
n
-punctured projective plane (
n\geq 3
), and the closed non-orientable surfaces.

\bullet
If
\Sigma
is an
n
-punctured sphere with
n\geq 4
, then
Q=\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\mid a_{1}\cdots a_{n}=1\rangle
.
Since
H
is CSA, there exists
i
such that
f(a_{i})
does not commute with
f(a_{i+1})
,
and we may assume
i=1
without loss of generality.
The subgroup
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
is the fundamental group of a pair of pants contained in
\Sigma
; it has non-abelian image
and its third boundary curve, whose fundamental group is generated by
a_{1}a_{2}
, is not pinched.

The fundamental group of the complement of this pair of pants is generated by
a_{3},\dots,a_{n}
and contains
a_{1}a_{2}
.
We are done if it has non-abelian image under
f
, so assume otherwise.
Then
f(a_{3}),\dots,f(a_{n}),f(a_{1}a_{2})
are non-trivial commuting elements.
Let
A
be the maximal abelian group containing
f(\langle a_{3},\dots,a_{n}\rangle)
(hence also
f(a_{1}a_{2})
).

We have
f(a_{2})\notin A
since otherwise
f(Q)=A
,
contradicting the non-abelianity of
f(Q)
.
By commutative transitivity,
f(a_{2})
does not commute with
f(a_{3})
, and similarly
f(a_{n})
does not commute with
f(a_{1})
.
We then let
\gamma
be a curve bounding
a pair of pants with fundamental group

\langle a_{2},a_{3}\rangle
.

\bullet
If
\Sigma
is an
n
-punctured projective plane with
n\geq 3
, we claim that there exists an embedded Möbius band
M\subset\Sigma

whose fundamental group is not killed by
f
.
If the claim is true, then one can write
\Sigma
as an
(n+1)
-punctured sphere
\Sigma^{\prime}
with
M
glued along a boundary component, which can be
viewed as
\Sigma^{\prime}
with a socket of index 2.
Since
n+1\geq 4
and
boundary components of
\Sigma^{\prime}
are not pinched,
one reduces
to the previous case using Remark
A.8
.

To prove the claim, consider a one-sided simple closed curve
\gamma
(whose regular neighborhood is a Möbius band),
and assume that
\pi_{1}(\gamma)
is killed.
Let
I
be an arc joining
\gamma
to a boundary component
b
. Then the concatenation
\gamma.I.b.\overline{I}

is homotopic to a simple closed curve which is one-sided, and whose image under
f
is conjugate to that of
b
hence is non-trivial.

\bullet
If
\Sigma
is a closed non-orientable surface (of genus at least 3 because its fundamental group has non-abelian image in
H
), one can write it as the union of an orientable surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
with one boundary component
and a Möbius band or a punctured Klein bottle. If
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains a non-pinched non-separating curve, we are done.
Otherwise, being generated by such curves,
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
has trivial image under
f
, so
f
factors through the fundamental group
of a projective plane or a Klein bottle. Since
H
is CSA, this implies that the image of
f
is abelian, a contradiction.
∎

A.5
Proof of Theorem
A.5

Consider the decomposition of the surface
\Sigma
(assumed to be non-exceptional) into pairs of pants and twice-punctured projective planes
given by Lemma
A.7
.
We first claim that, up to adding extra sockets, we may assume that all pieces of this decomposition are pairs of pants.

Indeed,
one can view a twice-punctured projective plane
P
as a pair of pants
P^{\prime}
with a Möbius band
glued to one of its boundary components; this corresponds to adding a socket of index 2 to
P^{\prime}
.
The fundamental group of the Möbius band (and of the corresponding boundary component of
P^{\prime}
) have non-trivial image by
f
since
otherwise
\pi_{1}(P)
would have abelian image.
By Remark
A.8
, the image of the fundamental group of
P^{\prime}
is also non-abelian.
The claim is then proved by replacing each twice-punctured projective plane by a pair of pants with sockets.

We thus have a decomposition of the space
X_{\hat{Q}}
into pairs of pants with sockets
P_{i}
whose fundamental groups have non-abelian
images under
f
. We are done if
f
is injective on every
\pi_{1}(P_{i})
, so
let
P=P_{i}
be a pair of pants with sockets such that
f
is not injective on
\pi_{1}(P)
.

If the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
is
-1
, then
\Sigma
is a once-punctured torus because it is assumed to be non-exceptional. This is a special case which will be treated below.
Otherwise, there exists a pair of pants
P^{\prime}\neq P
adjacent to
P
in the decomposition, and
P\cup P^{\prime}
is
a 4-punctured sphere (possibly with boundary components identified).
If we prove the theorem when
\Sigma
is either a once-punctured torus or a 4-punctured sphere, the general case follows by induction on the number of pairs of pants
P
such that
f
is not injective on
\pi_{1}(P)
.

\bullet
The punctured torus.

Let
d\geq 1
and let
\hat{Q}=\langle a,b,c\mid[a,b]=c^{d}\rangle
be the fundamental group of a punctured torus with a socket of index
d
(with
d=1
if there is no socket),
where the fundamental group
Q
of the punctured torus
\Sigma
is the subgroup
\langle a,b\rangle
, the curve
C
provided by Lemma
A.7

has fundamental group
\langle a\rangle
, and
cutting
\Sigma
along
C
produces a pair of pants
P
whose fundamental group
\langle a,bab^{-1}\rangle=\langle a,c^{d}\rangle=\langle bab^{-1},c^{d}\rangle
has non-abelian image.

Let
\gamma_{n}
be the curve obtained by twisting the curve representing
b

n
times around
C
.
Cutting the graph of spaces
X_{\hat{Q}}
representing
\hat{Q}
along
\gamma_{n}
creates a pair of pants with socket whose fundamental group is a free group
\hat{P}_{n}=\langle ba^{n},c\rangle
.

When applied to
(u,v,w)=(f(a),f(b),f(c))
, Corollary
A.13
, stated and proved in the next subsection, ensures that

f(\hat{P}_{n})
is free of rank 2 for all
n
large enough, so the restriction of
f
to
\hat{P}_{n}
is injective. This proves Theorem
A.5
when
\Sigma
is a once-punctured torus.

\bullet
The 4-punctured sphere.

Using Lemma
A.7
, we may assume that

G
has a presentation of the form

\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{4}\mid a_{1}^{d_{1}}a_{2}^{d_{2}}a_{3}^{d_{3}}a_{4}^{d_{4}}=1\rangle
, with
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)=\langle a_{1}^{d_{1}},\dots,a_{4}^{d_{4}}\rangle
, such that the simple closed curve
\gamma
represented by
g:=a_{1}^{d_{1}}a_{2}^{d_{2}}=(a_{3}^{d_{3}}a_{4}^{d_{4}})^{-1}
is not pinched by
f
, and the associated pairs of pants with sockets
\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle
and
\langle a_{3},a_{4}\rangle
have non-abelian image by
f
.
Note that
f(g)
does not commute with any
f(a_{i})
: if for instance it commutes with
f(a_{1})
,
then it also commutes with
f(a_{2}^{d_{2}})=f(a_{1}^{-d_{1}}g)
and
f(\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)
is abelian by commutative transitivity,
a contradiction.

We consider a second decomposition of
X_{\hat{Q}}
into two pairs of pants with sockets, corresponding to
P_{0}=\langle a_{2},a_{3}\rangle
and
P^{\prime}_{0}=\langle a_{4},a_{1}\rangle
. Twisting it
n
times around
\gamma
, we obtain a decomposition into
P_{n}=\langle a_{2},g^{n}a_{3}g^{-n}\rangle
and

P^{\prime}_{n}=\langle g^{n}a_{4}g^{-n},a_{1}\rangle
.
Since
f(g)
commutes with no
f(a_{i})
, Lemma
A.12
applies and ensures that
f
is injective on
P_{n}
and
P^{\prime}_{n}
for
n
large.

Remark A.10
.

To prove the theorem when
\hat{Q}
is the fundamental group of the closed exceptional surface
N_{3}
, one views
\hat{Q}
as a once-punctured torus with a socket of index
d=2
. One just has to check that
f
is injective on
the socket group. This is true
since otherwise
f
would factor by
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, the fundamental group of the closed torus,
and would therefore have abelian image.

A.6
Ping-pong lemmas

Let
H
be a
hyperbolic group (torsion is allowed). We fix a finite generating set and we let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the corresponding Cayley graph. Let
\bar{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}\cup\partial{\mathcal{G}}
be its compactification, obtained by adding the Gromov boundary
\partial{\mathcal{G}}=\partial H
(see
[
GdlH90
]
). The action of
H
on itself by left-translations extends to a continuous action on
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
.

For
g
of infinite order, we denote by
g^{\pm\infty}=\lim_{g\to\pm\infty}g^{n}

the attracting and repelling fixed points of
g
in
\partial{\mathcal{G}}
. It is a standard fact that
g
has North-South dynamics on

\bar{\mathcal{G}}
: given neighborhoods
W^{\pm}
of
g^{\pm\infty}
, one has
g^{n}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{-})\subset W^{+}
and
g^{-n}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{+})\subset W^{-}
for
n
large.
If
g,h
have infinite order and
\langle g,h\rangle
is not virtually cyclic,
the four points
g^{\pm\infty},h^{\pm\infty}
are distinct (this applies in particular if
H
is torsion-free and
g,h
do not commute).

The following lemmas are proved by standard arguments, but we have not found them in the literature.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
u,v,w\in H
, with
u,w
of infinite order (we allow
v=1
). Let
\xi^{+}=vu^{+\infty}
and
\xi^{-}=u^{-\infty}
. Assume that
\xi^{+},\xi^{-},w^{+\infty},w^{-\infty}
are distinct,
and
w^{i}\xi^{-}\neq\xi^{+}
for all integers
i
.
For
n
large enough,
\langle vu^{n},w\rangle
is a free subgroup freely generated by
vu^{n}
and
w
.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
u,v,w\in H
have infinite order. Assume that

\{w^{\pm\infty}\}\cap\{u^{\pm\infty}\}=\{w^{\pm\infty}\}\cap\{v^{\pm\infty}\}=\emptyset
(we allow
u^{\pm\infty}=v^{\pm\infty}
).
For
n
large enough,
\langle u,w^{n}vw^{-n}\rangle
is a free subgroup freely generated by
u
and
w^{n}vw^{-n}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.11
.

Let
x
be a basepoint in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Using North-South dynamics of
w
, we can find neighborhoods
W^{\pm}
of
\xi^{\pm}
in
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
such that the neighborhoods
w^{i}W^{\pm}
for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and the points
w^{j}x
for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are all disjoint.
Denoting
v_{n}=vu^{n}
, we have
v_{n}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{-})\subset W^{+}
and
v_{n}^{-1}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{+})\subset W^{-}
for
n
larger than some
n_{0}
: this follows from North-South dynamics of
u
, noting that
v^{-1}W^{+}
is a neighborhood of
u^{+\infty}
.
Note that, for
n>n_{0}
, one has

v_{n}^{i}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{-})\subset W^{+}
and
v_{n}^{-i}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W^{+})\subset W^{-}
for all
i>0
.
In particular,
v_{n}
has infinite order.

We can now play ping-pong. For
n>n_{0}
, consider an element

g=v_{n}^{n_{k}}w^{p_{k-1}}\cdots v_{n}^{n_{2}}w^{p_{1}}v_{n}^{n_{1}}w^{p_{0}}

in
\langle v_{n},w\rangle
, with all exponents nonzero and
k\geq 1
. Let
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm\}
be the sign of
n_{i}
. We show
gx\in W^{\varepsilon_{k}}
(this implies
gx\neq x
, hence
g\neq 1
).
The point
w^{p_{0}}x
is not in
W^{-\varepsilon_{1}}
, so
v_{n}^{n_{1}}w^{p_{0}}x\in W^{\varepsilon_{1}}
. We then have
w^{p_{1}}v_{n}^{n_{1}}w^{p_{0}}\in w^{p_{1}}W^{\varepsilon_{1}}
, so

w^{p_{1}}v_{n}^{n_{1}}w^{p_{0}}\notin W^{-\varepsilon_{2}}
and
v_{n}^{n_{2}}w^{n_{2}}v_{n}^{n_{1}}w^{p_{0}}\in W^{\varepsilon_{2}}
. Iterating gives the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.12
.

The proof is similar.
We choose neighborhoods
W^{\pm}
of
w^{\pm\infty}
in
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
such that
no
u^{i}x
belongs to
W^{+}
, the neighborhoods
u^{i}W^{+}
for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
are disjoint, and the neighborhoods
v^{i}W^{-}
are disjoint. For
n
larger than some
n_{0}
, we have
w^{n}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W_{-})\subset W_{+}

and
w^{-n}(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\setminus W_{+})\subset W_{-}
.

We consider an element

g=w^{n}v^{p_{k}}w^{-n}\cdots u^{n_{2}}w^{n}v^{p_{1}}w^{-n}u^{n_{1}}

in
\langle u,w^{n}vw^{-n}\rangle
and we show
gx\in W^{+}
(as above,
k\geq 1
and all exponents are nonzero).
We have
u^{n_{1}}x\notin W^{+}
, hence
w^{-n}u^{n_{1}}x\in W^{-}
, so
v^{p_{1}}w^{-n}u^{n_{1}}x\notin W^{-}
and
w^{n}v^{p_{1}}w^{-n}u^{n_{1}}x\in W^{+}
. Applying
u^{n_{2}}
takes the point outside of
W^{+}
and we can iterate.
∎

Corollary A.13
.

Let
H
be torsion-free. Let
u,v,w\in H
, with
u,w
non-trivial. Assume that
w
commutes with none of
u
or
vuv^{-1}
.
For
n
large enough,
\langle vu^{n},w\rangle
is a free subgroup freely generated by
vu^{n}
and
w
.

Proof.

We check that Lemma
A.11
applies. The points
w^{+\infty},w^{-\infty}
are distinct from
\xi^{-}=u^{-\infty}
and
\xi^{+}=(vuv^{-1})^{+\infty}
because
w
does not commute with

u
and
vuv^{-1}
.
Since
H
is torsion-free, no element exchanges the points
u^{+\infty}
and
u^{-\infty}
,
so
\xi^{+}
and
\xi^{-}
are not in the same
H
-orbit.
∎
